<li><a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_and_race" title="Woodrow Wilson and race">Wilson and race relations</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_House_(Washington,_D.C.)" title="Woodrow Wilson House (Washington, D.C.)">Wilson House</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Princeton_School_of_Public_and_International_Affairs" title="Princeton School of Public and International Affairs">Princeton School of Public and International Affairs</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_International_Center_for_Scholars" title="Woodrow Wilson International Center for Scholars">Wilson Center</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_Presidential_Library" title="Woodrow Wilson Presidential Library">Presidential Library</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Wilsonianism" title="Wilsonianism">Wilsonianism</a></li></ul>
<hr />
<span typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="Seal of the President of the United States" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Seal_of_the_President_of_the_United_States.svg/70px-Seal_of_the_President_of_the_United_States.svg.png" decoding="async" width="70" height="70" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Seal_of_the_President_of_the_United_States.svg/105px-Seal_of_the_President_of_the_United_States.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Seal_of_the_President_of_the_United_States.svg/140px-Seal_of_the_President_of_the_United_States.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="2424" data-file-height="2425" /></span></span></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Woodrow_Wilson_series" title="Template:Woodrow Wilson series"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Woodrow_Wilson_series" title="Template talk:Woodrow Wilson series"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Woodrow_Wilson_series&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Thomas Woodrow Wilson</b> (December 28, 1856&#160;&#8211;&#32;February 3, 1924) was an American politician and academic who served as the 28th <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">president of the United States</a> from 1913 to 1921. A member of the <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Democratic_Party_(United_States)" title="History of the Democratic Party (United States)">Democratic Party</a>, Wilson served as the <a href="/wiki/President_of_Princeton_University" class="mw-redirect" title="President of Princeton University">president of Princeton University</a> and as the <a href="/wiki/Governor_of_New_Jersey" title="Governor of New Jersey">governor of New Jersey</a> before winning the <a href="/wiki/1912_United_States_presidential_election" title="1912 United States presidential election">1912 presidential election</a>. As president, Wilson changed the nation's economic policies and led the United States into <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> in 1917. He was the leading architect of the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Nations" title="League of Nations">League of Nations</a>, and his progressive stance on foreign policy came to be known as <a href="/wiki/Wilsonianism" title="Wilsonianism">Wilsonianism</a>.
</p><p>Born in <a href="/wiki/Staunton,_Virginia" title="Staunton, Virginia">Staunton, Virginia</a>, Wilson <a href="/wiki/Early_life_of_Woodrow_Wilson" class="mw-redirect" title="Early life of Woodrow Wilson">grew up</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Southern_United_States" title="Southern United States">American South</a>, mainly in <a href="/wiki/Augusta,_Georgia" title="Augusta, Georgia">Augusta, Georgia</a>, during the <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_War" title="American Civil War">Civil War</a> and <a href="/wiki/Reconstruction_era" title="Reconstruction era">Reconstruction</a>. After earning a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Philosophy" title="Doctor of Philosophy">Ph.D.</a> in history and political science from <a href="/wiki/Johns_Hopkins_University" title="Johns Hopkins University">Johns Hopkins University</a>, Wilson taught at various colleges before becoming the president of <a href="/wiki/Princeton_University" title="Princeton University">Princeton University</a> and a spokesman for <a href="/wiki/Progressivism" title="Progressivism">progressivism</a> in higher education. As governor of New Jersey from 1911 to 1913, Wilson broke with party bosses and won the passage of several progressive reforms. To win the presidential nomination he mobilized progressives and Southerners to his cause at the <a href="/wiki/1912_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1912 Democratic National Convention">1912 Democratic National Convention</a>. Wilson defeated incumbent <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Republican_Party_(United_States)" title="History of the Republican Party (United States)">Republican</a> <a href="/wiki/William_Howard_Taft" title="William Howard Taft">William Howard Taft</a> and third-party nominee <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt" title="Theodore Roosevelt">Theodore Roosevelt</a> to easily win the 1912 United States presidential election, becoming the first Southerner to do so since 1848. During his first year as president, Wilson authorized the widespread <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_and_race" title="Woodrow Wilson and race">imposition of segregation</a> inside the federal bureaucracy. He ousted many African Americans from federal posts and his opposition to women's suffrage drew protests. His first term was largely devoted to pursuing passage of his progressive <a href="/wiki/The_New_Freedom" title="The New Freedom">New Freedom</a> domestic agenda. His first major priority was the <a href="/wiki/Revenue_Act_of_1913" title="Revenue Act of 1913">Revenue Act of 1913</a>, which lowered tariffs and began the modern <a href="/wiki/Income_tax_in_the_United_States" title="Income tax in the United States">income tax</a>. Wilson also negotiated the passage of the <a href="/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Act" title="Federal Reserve Act">Federal Reserve Act</a>, which created the <a href="/wiki/Federal_Reserve_System" class="mw-redirect" title="Federal Reserve System">Federal Reserve System</a>. Two major laws, the <a href="/wiki/Federal_Trade_Commission_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Federal Trade Commission Act">Federal Trade Commission Act</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Clayton_Antitrust_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Clayton Antitrust Act">Clayton Antitrust Act</a>, were enacted to promote business competition and combat extreme corporate power.
</p><p>At the <a href="/wiki/Outbreak_of_World_War_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Outbreak of World War I">outbreak of World War I</a> in 1914, the U.S. declared neutrality as Wilson tried to negotiate a peace between the <a href="/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I" title="Allies of World War I">Allied</a> and <a href="/wiki/Central_Powers" title="Central Powers">Central Powers</a>. He narrowly won re-election in the <a href="/wiki/1916_United_States_presidential_election" title="1916 United States presidential election">1916 United States presidential election</a>, boasting how he kept the nation out of wars in Europe and Mexico. In April 1917, Wilson asked Congress for a declaration of war against Germany in response to its policy of unrestricted submarine warfare that sank American merchant ships. Wilson nominally presided over war-time mobilization and left military matters to the generals. He instead concentrated on diplomacy, issuing the <i><a href="/wiki/Fourteen_Points" title="Fourteen Points">Fourteen Points</a></i> that the Allies and Germany accepted as a basis for post-war peace. He wanted the off-year elections of 1918 to be a referendum endorsing his policies, but instead the Republicans took control of Congress. After the <a href="/wiki/Armistice_of_11_November_1918" title="Armistice of 11 November 1918">Allied victory</a> in November 1918, Wilson went to Paris where he and the British and French leaders dominated the <a href="/wiki/Paris_Peace_Conference_(1919%E2%80%931920)" title="Paris Peace Conference (1919–1920)">Paris Peace Conference</a>. Wilson successfully advocated for the establishment of a multinational organization, the League of Nations. It was incorporated into the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles" title="Treaty of Versailles">Treaty of Versailles</a> that he signed. Wilson had refused to bring any leading Republican into the Paris talks, and back home he rejected a Republican compromise that would have allowed the Senate to ratify the Versailles Treaty and join the League.
</p><p>Wilson had intended to seek a third term in office but suffered a severe stroke in October 1919 that left him incapacitated. His wife and his doctor controlled Wilson, and no significant decisions were made. Meanwhile, his policies alienated German- and Irish-American Democrats and the Republicans won a landslide in the 1920 presidential election. Scholars have generally <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">ranked</a> Wilson in the upper tier of U.S. presidents, although he has been criticized for supporting <a href="/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States" title="Racial segregation in the United States">racial segregation</a>. His liberalism nevertheless lives on as a major factor in American foreign policy, and his vision of ethnic self-determination resonated globally.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Early_life_and_academic_career_of_Woodrow_Wilson" title="Early life and academic career of Woodrow Wilson">Early life and academic career of Woodrow Wilson</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Woodrow_Wilson_by_Pach_Bros_c1875.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/Woodrow_Wilson_by_Pach_Bros_c1875.jpg/220px-Woodrow_Wilson_by_Pach_Bros_c1875.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="275" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/Woodrow_Wilson_by_Pach_Bros_c1875.jpg/330px-Woodrow_Wilson_by_Pach_Bros_c1875.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/Woodrow_Wilson_by_Pach_Bros_c1875.jpg/440px-Woodrow_Wilson_by_Pach_Bros_c1875.jpg 2x" data-file-width="906" data-file-height="1132" /></a><figcaption>Wilson, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> mid-1870s</figcaption></figure>
<p>Thomas Woodrow Wilson was born to a family of <a href="/wiki/Scotch-Irish_Americans" title="Scotch-Irish Americans">Scots-Irish</a> and <a href="/wiki/Scottish_Americans" title="Scottish Americans">Scottish descent</a> in <a href="/wiki/Staunton,_Virginia" title="Staunton, Virginia">Staunton, Virginia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He was the third of four children and the first son of <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Ruggles_Wilson" title="Joseph Ruggles Wilson">Joseph Ruggles Wilson</a> and Jessie Janet Woodrow. Wilson's paternal grandparents had immigrated to the United States from <a href="/wiki/Strabane" title="Strabane">Strabane</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Tyrone" title="County Tyrone">County Tyrone</a>, Ireland, in 1807, and settled in <a href="/wiki/Steubenville,_Ohio" title="Steubenville, Ohio">Steubenville, Ohio</a>. Wilson's paternal grandfather <a href="/wiki/James_Wilson_(journalist)" title="James Wilson (journalist)">James Wilson</a> published a pro-<a href="/wiki/Tariff" title="Tariff">tariff</a> and <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States" title="Abolitionism in the United States">anti-slavery</a> newspaper, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Western_Herald_and_Gazette" class="mw-redirect" title="The Western Herald and Gazette">The Western Herald and Gazette</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Wilson's maternal grandfather, Reverend Thomas Woodrow, moved from <a href="/wiki/Paisley,_Renfrewshire" title="Paisley, Renfrewshire">Paisley, Renfrewshire</a>, Scotland, to <a href="/wiki/Carlisle,_Cumbria" class="mw-redirect" title="Carlisle, Cumbria">Carlisle, Cumbria</a>, England, before migrating to <a href="/wiki/Chillicothe,_Ohio" title="Chillicothe, Ohio">Chillicothe, Ohio</a>, in the late 1830s.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Joseph met Jessie while she was attending a girl's academy in Steubenville, and the two married on June 7, 1849. Soon after the wedding, Joseph was ordained as a <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian" class="mw-redirect" title="Presbyterian">Presbyterian</a> pastor and assigned to serve in Staunton.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> His son Woodrow was born in <a href="/wiki/The_Manse_(Woodrow_Wilson)" class="mw-redirect" title="The Manse (Woodrow Wilson)">the Manse</a>, a house in the Staunton First Presbyterian Church where Joseph served. Before he was two, the family moved to <a href="/wiki/Augusta,_Georgia" title="Augusta, Georgia">Augusta, Georgia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-0&#39;Toole_2018_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-0&#39;Toole_2018-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson's earliest memory of his early youth was of playing in his yard and standing near the front gate of the Augusta parsonage at the age of three, when he heard a passerby announce in disgust that <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln" title="Abraham Lincoln">Abraham Lincoln</a> had <a href="/wiki/1860_United_States_presidential_election" title="1860 United States presidential election">been elected</a> and that a <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_War" title="American Civil War">war</a> was coming.<sup id="cite_ref-0&#39;Toole_2018_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-0&#39;Toole_2018-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Wilson was one of only two <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">U.S. presidents</a> to be a citizen of the <a href="/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America" title="Confederate States of America">Confederate States of America</a>; the other was <a href="/wiki/John_Tyler" title="John Tyler">John Tyler</a>, who <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_John_Tyler" title="Presidency of John Tyler">served as the nation's tenth president</a> from 1841 to 1845. Wilson's father identified with the <a href="/wiki/Southern_United_States" title="Southern United States">Southern United States</a> and was a staunch supporter of the Confederacy during the <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_War" title="American Civil War">American Civil War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson's father was one of the founders of the Southern <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian_Church_in_the_United_States" title="Presbyterian Church in the United States">Presbyterian Church in the United States</a> (PCUS) following its 1861 split from the Northern Presbyterians. He became minister of the <a href="/wiki/First_Presbyterian_Church_(Augusta,_Georgia)" title="First Presbyterian Church (Augusta, Georgia)">First Presbyterian Church</a> in Augusta, and the family lived there until 1870.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> From 1870 to 1874, Wilson lived in <a href="/wiki/Columbia,_South_Carolina" title="Columbia, South Carolina">Columbia, South Carolina</a>, where his father was a theology professor at the <a href="/wiki/Columbia_Theological_Seminary" title="Columbia Theological Seminary">Columbia Theological Seminary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> In 1873, Wilson became a communicant member of the <a href="/wiki/Columbia_First_Presbyterian_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Columbia First Presbyterian Church">Columbia First Presbyterian Church</a>; he remained a member throughout his life.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson attended <a href="/wiki/Davidson_College" title="Davidson College">Davidson College</a> in <a href="/wiki/Davidson,_North_Carolina" title="Davidson, North Carolina">Davidson, North Carolina</a> in the 1873–74 school year but transferred as a freshman to the College of New Jersey, which is now <a href="/wiki/Princeton_University" title="Princeton University">Princeton University</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> where he studied <a href="/wiki/Political_philosophy" title="Political philosophy">political philosophy</a> and <a href="/wiki/History" title="History">history</a>, joined the <a href="/wiki/Phi_Kappa_Psi" title="Phi Kappa Psi">Phi Kappa Psi</a> fraternity, and was active in the <a href="/wiki/Whig_literary_and_debating_society" class="mw-redirect" title="Whig literary and debating society">Whig literary and debating society</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> He was also elected secretary of the school's <a href="/wiki/American_football" title="American football">football</a> association, president of the school's <a href="/wiki/Baseball" title="Baseball">baseball</a> association, and managing editor of the student newspaper.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> In the hotly contested <a href="/wiki/1876_United_States_presidential_election" title="1876 United States presidential election">presidential election of 1876</a>, Wilson supported the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)" title="Democratic Party (United States)">Democratic Party</a> and its nominee, <a href="/wiki/Samuel_J._Tilden" title="Samuel J. Tilden">Samuel J. Tilden</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After graduating from Princeton in 1879,<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Wilson attended the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Virginia_School_of_Law" title="University of Virginia School of Law">University of Virginia School of Law</a> in <a href="/wiki/Charlottesville,_Virginia" title="Charlottesville, Virginia">Charlottesville, Virginia</a>, where he was involved in the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_Glee_Club" title="Virginia Glee Club">Virginia Glee Club</a> and served as president of the <a href="/wiki/Jefferson_Literary_and_Debating_Society" title="Jefferson Literary and Debating Society">Jefferson Literary and Debating Society</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Poor health forced Wilson to withdraw from law school, but he continued to study law on his own while living with his parents in <a href="/wiki/Wilmington,_North_Carolina" title="Wilmington, North Carolina">Wilmington, North Carolina</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Wilson was admitted to the <a href="/wiki/Georgia_bar" class="mw-redirect" title="Georgia bar">Georgia bar</a> and made a brief attempt at establishing a <a href="/wiki/Law_firm" title="Law firm">law firm</a> in <a href="/wiki/Atlanta" title="Atlanta">Atlanta</a> in 1882.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Though he found legal history and substantive jurisprudence interesting, he abhorred the day-to-day procedural aspects of the practice of law. After less than a year, Wilson abandoned his legal practice to pursue the study of political science and history.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In late 1883, Wilson enrolled at the recently established <a href="/wiki/Johns_Hopkins_University" title="Johns Hopkins University">Johns Hopkins University</a> in <a href="/wiki/Baltimore" title="Baltimore">Baltimore</a> for doctoral studies.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Built on the <a href="/wiki/Humboldtian_model_of_higher_education" title="Humboldtian model of higher education">Humboldtian model of higher education</a>, Johns Hopkins was inspired particularly from historic <a href="/wiki/Heidelberg_University" title="Heidelberg University">Heidelberg University</a> in <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a> and was committed to research as central to its academic and institutional mission. Wilson studied history, political science, <a href="/wiki/German_language" title="German language">German</a>, and other fields.<sup id="cite_ref-Pestritto_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pestritto-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Wilson hoped to become a professor, writing that "a professorship was the only feasible place for me, the only place that would afford leisure for reading and for original work, the only strictly literary berth with an income attached."<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson spent much of his time at Johns Hopkins University writing <i>Congressional Government: A Study in American Politics</i>, which grew out of a series of essays in which he examined the workings of the federal government.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> In 1886, Wilson was awarded a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Philosophy" title="Doctor of Philosophy">Ph.D.</a> in history and government from Johns Hopkins University,<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> making him the only U.S. president in the nation's history to possess a Ph.D.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> In early 1885, <a href="/wiki/Houghton_Mifflin_Harcourt" title="Houghton Mifflin Harcourt">Houghton Mifflin</a> published Wilson's <i>Congressional Government</i>, which was well received with one critic called it "the best critical writing on the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_the_United_States" title="Constitution of the United States">American constitution</a> which has appeared since the <a href="/wiki/Federalist_Papers" class="mw-redirect" title="Federalist Papers">'Federalist' papers</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Marriage_and_family">Marriage and family</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ELWilson.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/ELWilson.jpg/220px-ELWilson.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="286" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/ELWilson.jpg/330px-ELWilson.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/ELWilson.jpg/440px-ELWilson.jpg 2x" data-file-width="863" data-file-height="1123" /></a><figcaption>In September 1883, Wilson proposed to his future wife, <a href="/wiki/Ellen_Axson_Wilson" title="Ellen Axson Wilson">Ellen Axson Wilson</a>, the daughter of a <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian" class="mw-redirect" title="Presbyterian">Presbyterian</a> minister in <a href="/wiki/Savannah,_Georgia" title="Savannah, Georgia">Savannah, Georgia</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1883, Wilson met and fell in love with <a href="/wiki/Ellen_Louise_Axson" class="mw-redirect" title="Ellen Louise Axson">Ellen Louise Axson</a>, the daughter of a <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian" class="mw-redirect" title="Presbyterian">Presbyterian</a> minister from <a href="/wiki/Savannah,_Georgia" title="Savannah, Georgia">Savannah, Georgia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> He proposed marriage in September 1883; she accepted, but they agreed to postpone marriage while Wilson attended graduate school.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Axson graduated from <a href="/wiki/Art_Students_League_of_New_York" title="Art Students League of New York">Art Students League of New York</a>, worked in portraiture, and received a medal for one of her works from the <a href="/wiki/Exposition_Universelle_(1878)" title="Exposition Universelle (1878)">Exposition Universelle (1878)</a> in Paris.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> She agreed to sacrifice further independent artistic pursuits in order to marry Wilson in 1885.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ellen learned <a href="/wiki/German_language" title="German language">German</a> so she could help translate German-language political science publications relevant to Woodrow's research.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 1886, the couple's first child, <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Woodrow_Wilson" title="Margaret Woodrow Wilson">Margaret</a>, was born. Their second child, <a href="/wiki/Jessie_Woodrow_Wilson_Sayre" title="Jessie Woodrow Wilson Sayre">Jessie</a>, was born in August 1887.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Their third and final child, <a href="/wiki/Eleanor_Wilson_McAdoo" title="Eleanor Wilson McAdoo">Eleanor</a>, was born in October 1889.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> In 1913, Jessie married <a href="/wiki/Francis_Bowes_Sayre_Sr." title="Francis Bowes Sayre Sr.">Francis Bowes Sayre Sr.</a>, who later served as <a href="/wiki/High_Commissioner_to_the_Philippines" title="High Commissioner to the Philippines">High Commissioner to the Philippines</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> In 1914, their third child Eleanor married <a href="/wiki/William_Gibbs_McAdoo" title="William Gibbs McAdoo">William Gibbs McAdoo</a>, <a href="/wiki/U.S._Secretary_of_the_Treasury" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Secretary of the Treasury">U.S. Secretary of the Treasury</a> under Woodrow Wilson and later a <a href="/wiki/U.S._Senator_from_California" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Senator from California">U.S. Senator from California</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-auto1_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto1-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Academic_career">Academic career</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Professor">Professor</span></h3>
<p>From 1885 to 1888, Wilson taught at <a href="/wiki/Bryn_Mawr_College" title="Bryn Mawr College">Bryn Mawr College</a>, a newly established <a href="/wiki/Women%27s_colleges_in_the_United_States" title="Women&#39;s colleges in the United States">women's college</a> in <a href="/wiki/Bryn_Mawr,_Pennsylvania" title="Bryn Mawr, Pennsylvania">Bryn Mawr, Pennsylvania</a>, outside <a href="/wiki/Philadelphia" title="Philadelphia">Philadelphia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Wilson taught ancient Greek and Roman history, American history, political science, and other subjects. At the time, there were only 42 students at the college, nearly all of them too passive for his taste. <a href="/wiki/M._Carey_Thomas" title="M. Carey Thomas">M. Carey Thomas</a>, the dean, was an aggressive feminist and Wilson clashed with her over his contract, resulting in a bitter dispute. In 1888, Wilson left Bryn Mawr College, and was not given a farewell.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson accepted a position at <a href="/wiki/Wesleyan_University" title="Wesleyan University">Wesleyan University</a>, an elite undergraduate college for men in <a href="/wiki/Middletown,_Connecticut" title="Middletown, Connecticut">Middletown, Connecticut</a>, where he coached the <a href="/wiki/American_football" title="American football">football</a> team, founded a debate team, and taught graduate courses in political economy and <a href="/wiki/Western_history" class="mw-redirect" title="Western history">Western history</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 1890, with the help of friends, Wilson was appointed Chair of Jurisprudence and Political Economy at the <a href="/wiki/Princeton_University" title="Princeton University">College of New Jersey</a>, at an annual salary of $3,000 (equivalent to $97,711&#32;in 2022).<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Wilson quickly earned a reputation at Princeton as a compelling speaker.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> In 1896, <a href="/wiki/Francis_Landey_Patton" title="Francis Landey Patton">Francis Landey Patton</a> announced that College of New Jersey was being renamed Princeton University; an ambitious program of expansion for the university accompanied the name change.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/1896_United_States_presidential_election" title="1896 United States presidential election">1896 presidential election</a>, Wilson rejected Democratic nominee <a href="/wiki/William_Jennings_Bryan" title="William Jennings Bryan">William Jennings Bryan</a> as too far to the left and instead supported the conservative "<a href="/wiki/Gold_Democrat" class="mw-redirect" title="Gold Democrat">Gold Democrat</a>" nominee, <a href="/wiki/John_M._Palmer_(politician)" title="John M. Palmer (politician)">John M. Palmer</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Wilson's academic reputation continued to grow throughout the 1890s, and he turned down multiple positions elsewhere,, including at <a href="/wiki/Johns_Hopkins_University" title="Johns Hopkins University">Johns Hopkins University</a> and the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Virginia" title="University of Virginia">University of Virginia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At Princeton University, Wilson published several works of history and political science and was a regular contributor to <i><a href="/wiki/Political_Science_Quarterly" title="Political Science Quarterly">Political Science Quarterly</a></i>. Wilson's textbook, <i>The State</i>, was widely used in American college courses until the 1920s.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> In <i>The State</i>, Wilson wrote that governments could legitimately promote the general welfare "by forbidding child labor, by supervising the sanitary conditions of factories, by limiting the employment of women in occupations hurtful to their health, by instituting official tests of the purity or the quality of goods sold, by limiting the hours of labor in certain trades, [and] by a hundred and one limitations of the power of unscrupulous or heartless men to out-do the scrupulous and merciful in trade or industry."<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> He also wrote that charity efforts should be removed from the private domain and "made the imperative legal duty of the whole," a position which, according to historian Robert M. Saunders, seemed to indicate that Wilson "was laying the groundwork for the modern welfare state."<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> His third book, <i>Division and Reunion</i> (1893),<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> became a standard university textbook for teaching mid- and late-19th century U.S. history.<sup id="cite_ref-auto2_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="President_of_Princeton_University">President of Princeton University</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Princeton_University#Woodrow_Wilson" title="History of Princeton University">History of Princeton University §&#160;Woodrow Wilson</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Woodrow_Wilson_1902_cph.3b11773.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Woodrow_Wilson_1902_cph.3b11773.jpg/220px-Woodrow_Wilson_1902_cph.3b11773.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="312" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Woodrow_Wilson_1902_cph.3b11773.jpg/330px-Woodrow_Wilson_1902_cph.3b11773.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Woodrow_Wilson_1902_cph.3b11773.jpg/440px-Woodrow_Wilson_1902_cph.3b11773.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1050" data-file-height="1491" /></a><figcaption>Wilson in 1902</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Princeton_University_Prospect.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Princeton_University_Prospect.jpg/220px-Princeton_University_Prospect.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Princeton_University_Prospect.jpg/330px-Princeton_University_Prospect.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Princeton_University_Prospect.jpg/440px-Princeton_University_Prospect.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="960" /></a><figcaption>As president of <a href="/wiki/Princeton_University" title="Princeton University">Princeton University</a>, Wilson lived in Prospect House on the university campus.</figcaption></figure> 
<p>In June 1902, Princeton trustees promoted Professor Wilson to president, replacing Patton, whom the trustees perceived to be an inefficient administrator.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Wilson aspired, as he told alumni, "to transform thoughtless boys performing tasks into thinking men." He tried to raise admission standards and to replace the "gentleman's C" with serious study. To emphasize the development of expertise, Wilson instituted academic departments and a system of core requirements. Students were to meet in groups of six under the guidance of teaching assistants known as <a href="/wiki/Preceptor_in_education" class="mw-redirect" title="Preceptor in education">preceptors</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (February 2019)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> To fund these new programs, Wilson undertook an ambitious and successful fundraising campaign, convincing alumni such as <a href="/wiki/Moses_Taylor_Pyne" title="Moses Taylor Pyne">Moses Taylor Pyne</a> and philanthropists such as <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Carnegie" title="Andrew Carnegie">Andrew Carnegie</a> to donate to the school.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Wilson appointed the first Jew and the first Roman Catholic to the faculty, and helped liberate the board from domination by conservative Presbyterians.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> He also worked to keep African Americans out of the school, even as other <a href="/wiki/Ivy_League" title="Ivy League">Ivy League</a> schools were accepting small numbers of black people.<sup id="cite_ref-O&#39;Reilly1997_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-O&#39;Reilly1997-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson's efforts to reform Princeton earned him national notoriety, but they also took a toll on his health.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> In 1906, Wilson awoke to find himself blind in the left eye, the result of a blood clot and hypertension. Modern medical opinion surmises Wilson had had a stroke; he later was diagnosed, as his father had been, with <a href="/wiki/Hardening_of_the_arteries" class="mw-redirect" title="Hardening of the arteries">hardening of the arteries</a>. He began to exhibit his father's traits of impatience and intolerance, which would on occasion lead to errors of judgment.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1906, while vacationing in <a href="/wiki/Bermuda" title="Bermuda">Bermuda</a>, Wilson met Mary Hulbert Peck, a socialite. According to biographer <a href="/wiki/August_Heckscher_II" title="August Heckscher II">August Heckscher II</a>, Wilson's friendship with Peck became the topic of frank discussion between Wilson and his wife, although Wilson historians have not conclusively established there was an affair.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Wilson also sent very personal letters to her,<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> which were later used against him by his adversaries.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Having reorganized Princeton University's curriculum and established the preceptorial system, Wilson next attempted to curtail the influence of social elites at Princeton by abolishing the upper-class <a href="/wiki/Eating_club" class="mw-redirect" title="Eating club">eating clubs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> He proposed moving the students into colleges, also known as quadrangles, but Wilson's plan was met with fierce opposition from Princeton alumni.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> In October 1907, due to the intensity of alumni opposition, Princeton's board of trustees instructed Wilson to withdraw his plan for relocating student dormitories.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Late in his tenure, Wilson had a confrontation with <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Fleming_West" title="Andrew Fleming West">Andrew Fleming West</a>, dean of Princeton University's graduate school and his ally, ex-President <a href="/wiki/Grover_Cleveland" title="Grover Cleveland">Grover Cleveland</a>, who was a Princeton trustee. Wilson wanted to integrate a proposed graduate school building into the core of the campus, but West preferred a more distant campus site. In 1909, Princeton's board accepted a gift made to the graduate school campaign subject to the graduate school being located off campus.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson became disenchanted with his job as Princeton University president due to the resistance to his recommendations, and he began considering a run for political office. Prior to the <a href="/wiki/1908_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1908 Democratic National Convention">1908 Democratic National Convention</a>, Wilson dropped hints to some influential players in the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)" title="Democratic Party (United States)">Democratic Party</a> of his interest in the ticket. While he had no real expectations of being placed on it, Wilson left instructions that he should not be offered the vice presidential nomination. Party regulars considered his ideas politically and geographically detached and fanciful, but the seeds of interest had been sown.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> In 1956, <a href="/wiki/McGeorge_Bundy" title="McGeorge Bundy">McGeorge Bundy</a> described Wilson's contribution to Princeton: "Wilson was right in his conviction that Princeton must be more than a wonderfully pleasant and decent home for nice young men; it has been more ever since his time."<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Governor_of_New_Jersey_.281911.E2.80.931913.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Governor_of_New_Jersey_(1911–1913)">Governor of New Jersey (1911–1913)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/1910_New_Jersey_gubernatorial_election" title="1910 New Jersey gubernatorial election">1910 New Jersey gubernatorial election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Woodrow_Wilson,_New_Jersey_Governor_-_1911.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Woodrow_Wilson%2C_New_Jersey_Governor_-_1911.jpg/220px-Woodrow_Wilson%2C_New_Jersey_Governor_-_1911.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Woodrow_Wilson%2C_New_Jersey_Governor_-_1911.jpg/330px-Woodrow_Wilson%2C_New_Jersey_Governor_-_1911.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Woodrow_Wilson%2C_New_Jersey_Governor_-_1911.jpg/440px-Woodrow_Wilson%2C_New_Jersey_Governor_-_1911.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2448" data-file-height="3264" /></a><figcaption>Wilson as <a href="/wiki/Governor_of_New_Jersey" title="Governor of New Jersey">New Jersey governor</a> in 1911</figcaption></figure>
<p>By January 1910, Wilson had drawn the attention of <a href="/wiki/James_Smith_Jr." title="James Smith Jr.">James Smith Jr.</a> and <a href="/wiki/George_Brinton_McClellan_Harvey" title="George Brinton McClellan Harvey">George Brinton McClellan Harvey</a>, two leaders of New Jersey's Democratic Party, as a potential candidate in the upcoming <a href="/wiki/1910_New_Jersey_gubernatorial_election" title="1910 New Jersey gubernatorial election">gubernatorial election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> Having lost the last five gubernatorial elections, New Jersey Democratic leaders decided to throw their support behind Wilson, an untested and unconventional candidate. Party leaders believed that Wilson's academic reputation made him the ideal spokesman against <a href="/wiki/Trust_(business)" title="Trust (business)">trusts</a> and corruption, but they also hoped his inexperience in governing would make him easy to influence.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> Wilson agreed to accept the nomination if "it came to me unsought, unanimously, and without pledges to anybody about anything."<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the state party convention, the bosses marshaled their forces and won the nomination for Wilson. On October 20, Wilson submitted his letter of resignation to <a href="/wiki/Princeton_University" title="Princeton University">Princeton University</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Wilson's campaign focused on his promise to be independent of party bosses. He quickly shed his professorial style for more emboldened speechmaking and presented himself as a full-fledged <a href="/wiki/Progressivism_in_the_United_States" title="Progressivism in the United States">progressive</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Though Republican <a href="/wiki/William_Howard_Taft" title="William Howard Taft">William Howard Taft</a> had carried New Jersey in the <a href="/wiki/1908_United_States_presidential_election" title="1908 United States presidential election">1908 presidential election</a> by more than 82,000 votes, Wilson soundly defeated Republican gubernatorial nominee <a href="/wiki/Vivian_M._Lewis" title="Vivian M. Lewis">Vivian M. Lewis</a> by a margin of more than 65,000 votes.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Democrats also took control of the <a href="/wiki/New_Jersey_General_Assembly" title="New Jersey General Assembly">general assembly</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1910_United_States_elections" title="1910 United States elections">1910 elections</a>, though the <a href="/wiki/New_Jersey_Senate" title="New Jersey Senate">state senate</a> remained in Republican hands.<sup id="cite_ref-heckscher220_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-heckscher220-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> After winning the election, Wilson appointed <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Patrick_Tumulty" title="Joseph Patrick Tumulty">Joseph Patrick Tumulty</a> as his private secretary, a position he held throughout Wilson's political career.<sup id="cite_ref-heckscher220_75-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-heckscher220-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson began formulating his reformist agenda, intending to ignore the demands of his party machinery. Smith asked Wilson to endorse his bid for the U.S. Senate, but Wilson refused and instead endorsed Smith's opponent <a href="/wiki/James_Edgar_Martine" class="mw-redirect" title="James Edgar Martine">James Edgar Martine</a>, who had won the Democratic primary. Martine's victory in the Senate election helped Wilson position himself as an independent force in the New Jersey Democratic Party.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> By the time Wilson took office, New Jersey had gained a reputation for public corruption; the state was known as the "Mother of Trusts" because it allowed companies like <a href="/wiki/Standard_Oil" title="Standard Oil">Standard Oil</a> to escape the <a href="/wiki/United_States_antitrust_law" title="United States antitrust law">antitrust laws</a> of other states.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Wilson and his allies quickly won passage of the Geran bill, which undercut the power of the political bosses by requiring primaries for all elective offices and party officials. A corrupt practices law and a workmen's compensation statute that Wilson supported won passage shortly thereafter.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> For his success in passing these laws during the first months of his gubernatorial term, Wilson won national and bipartisan recognition as a reformer and a leader of the Progressive movement.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Republicans took control of the state assembly in early 1912, and Wilson spent much of the rest of his tenure vetoing bills.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> He nonetheless won passage of laws that restricted labor by women and children and increased standards for factory working conditions.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> A new State Board of Education was set up "with the power to conduct inspections and enforce standards, regulate districts' borrowing authority, and require special classes for students with handicaps."<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Before leaving office Wilson oversaw the establishment of free dental clinics and enacted a "comprehensive and scientific" poor law. Trained nursing was standardized, while contract labor in all reformatories and prisons was abolished and an indeterminate sentence act passed.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> A law was introduced that compelled all railroad companies "to pay their employees twice monthly," while regulation of the working hours, health, safety, employment, and age of people employed in mercantile establishments was carried out.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Shortly before leaving office, Wilson signed a series of antitrust laws known as the "Seven Sisters," as well as another law that removed the power to select <a href="/wiki/Juries_in_the_United_States" title="Juries in the United States">juries</a> from local sheriffs.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidential_election_of_1912">Presidential election of 1912</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1912_United_States_presidential_election" title="1912 United States presidential election">1912 United States presidential election</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Democratic_nomination">Democratic nomination</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/1912_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries" title="1912 Democratic Party presidential primaries">1912 Democratic Party presidential primaries</a> and <a href="/wiki/1912_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1912 Democratic National Convention">1912 Democratic National Convention</a></div>
<p>Wilson became a prominent 1912 presidential contender immediately upon his election as <a href="/wiki/Governor_of_New_Jersey" title="Governor of New Jersey">Governor of New Jersey</a> in 1910, and his clashes with state party bosses enhanced his reputation with the rising Progressive movement.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> In addition to progressives, Wilson enjoyed the support of Princeton alumni such as <a href="/wiki/Cyrus_McCormick" title="Cyrus McCormick">Cyrus McCormick</a> and Southerners such as <a href="/wiki/Walter_Hines_Page" title="Walter Hines Page">Walter Hines Page</a>, who believed that Wilson's status as a transplanted Southerner gave him broad appeal.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Though Wilson's shift to the left won the admiration of many, it also created enemies such as <a href="/wiki/George_Brinton_McClellan_Harvey" title="George Brinton McClellan Harvey">George Brinton McClellan Harvey</a>, a former Wilson supporter who had close ties to <a href="/wiki/Wall_Street" title="Wall Street">Wall Street</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> In July 1911, Wilson brought <a href="/wiki/William_Gibbs_McAdoo" title="William Gibbs McAdoo">William Gibbs McAdoo</a> and "Colonel" <a href="/wiki/Edward_M._House" title="Edward M. House">Edward M. House</a> in to manage the campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Prior to the <a href="/wiki/1912_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1912 Democratic National Convention">1912 Democratic National Convention</a>, Wilson made a special effort to win the approval of three-time Democratic presidential nominee <a href="/wiki/William_Jennings_Bryan" title="William Jennings Bryan">William Jennings Bryan</a>, whose followers had largely dominated the Democratic Party since the <a href="/wiki/1896_United_States_presidential_election" title="1896 United States presidential election">1896 presidential election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Speaker of the House <a href="/wiki/Champ_Clark" title="Champ Clark">Champ Clark</a> of Missouri was viewed by many as the front-runner for the nomination, while House Majority Leader <a href="/wiki/Oscar_Underwood" title="Oscar Underwood">Oscar Underwood</a> of Alabama also loomed as a challenger. Clark found support among the Bryan wing of the party, while Underwood appealed to the conservative <a href="/wiki/Bourbon_Democrat" title="Bourbon Democrat">Bourbon Democrats</a>, especially in the South.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/1912_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries" title="1912 Democratic Party presidential primaries">1912 Democratic Party presidential primaries</a>, Clark won several of the early contests, but Wilson finished strong with victories in Texas, the Northeast, and the Midwest.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> On the first presidential ballot of the Democratic convention, Clark won a plurality of delegates; his support continued to grow after the New York <a href="/wiki/Tammany_Hall" title="Tammany Hall">Tammany Hall</a> machine swung behind him on the tenth ballot.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> Tammany's support backfired for Clark, as Bryan announced that he would not support any candidate that had Tammany's backing, and Clark began losing delegates on subsequent ballots.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Wilson gained the support of <a href="/wiki/Roger_Charles_Sullivan" title="Roger Charles Sullivan">Roger Charles Sullivan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Taggart" title="Thomas Taggart">Thomas Taggart</a> by promising the vice presidency to Governor <a href="/wiki/Thomas_R._Marshall" title="Thomas R. Marshall">Thomas R. Marshall</a> of Indiana.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> and several Southern delegations shifted their support from Underwood to Wilson. Wilson finally won two-thirds of the vote on the convention's 46th ballot, and Marshall became Wilson's running mate.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="General_election">General election</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1912.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/ElectoralCollege1912.svg/220px-ElectoralCollege1912.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="128" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/ElectoralCollege1912.svg/330px-ElectoralCollege1912.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/ElectoralCollege1912.svg/440px-ElectoralCollege1912.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1020" data-file-height="593" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/1912_United_States_presidential_election" title="1912 United States presidential election">1912 presidential electoral college</a> map</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the 1912 general election, Wilson faced two major opponents: one-term Republican incumbent <a href="/wiki/William_Howard_Taft" title="William Howard Taft">William Howard Taft</a>, and former Republican President <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt" title="Theodore Roosevelt">Theodore Roosevelt</a>, who ran a <a href="/wiki/Third_party_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Third party (United States)">third party</a> campaign as the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Party_(United_States,_1912)" class="mw-redirect" title="Progressive Party (United States, 1912)">"Bull Moose" Party</a> nominee. The fourth candidate was <a href="/wiki/Eugene_V._Debs" title="Eugene V. Debs">Eugene V. Debs</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Socialist_Party_of_America" title="Socialist Party of America">Socialist Party</a>. Roosevelt had broken with his former party at the <a href="/wiki/1912_Republican_National_Convention" title="1912 Republican National Convention">1912 Republican National Convention</a> after Taft narrowly won re-nomination, and the split in the Republican Party made Democrats hopeful that they could win the presidency for the first time since the <a href="/wiki/1892_United_States_presidential_election" title="1892 United States presidential election">1892 presidential election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Roosevelt emerged as Wilson's main challenger, and Wilson and Roosevelt largely campaigned against each other despite sharing similarly progressive platforms that called for an interventionist central government.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Wilson directed campaign finance chairman <a href="/wiki/Henry_Morgenthau,_Sr." class="mw-redirect" title="Henry Morgenthau, Sr.">Henry Morgenthau</a> not to accept contributions from corporations and to prioritize smaller donations from the widest possible quarters of the public.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> During the election campaign, Wilson asserted that it was the task of government "to make those adjustments of life which will put every man in a position to claim his normal rights as a living, human being."<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> With the help of legal scholar <a href="/wiki/Louis_Brandeis" title="Louis Brandeis">Louis Brandeis</a>, he developed his <a href="/wiki/The_New_Freedom" title="The New Freedom">New Freedom</a> platform, focusing especially on breaking up trusts and lowering <a href="/wiki/Tariffs_in_United_States_history" class="mw-redirect" title="Tariffs in United States history">tariff</a> rates.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Brandeis and Wilson rejected Roosevelt's proposal to establish a powerful bureaucracy charged with regulating large corporations, instead favoring the break-up of large corporations in order to create a level economic playing field.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson engaged in a spirited campaign, criss-crossing the country to deliver numerous speeches.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Ultimately, he took 42 percent of the popular vote and 435 of the 531 <a href="/wiki/Electoral_College_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Electoral College (United States)">electoral votes</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> Roosevelt won most of the remaining electoral votes and 27.4 percent of the popular vote, one of the <a href="/wiki/List_of_third_party_performances_in_United_States_elections" class="mw-redirect" title="List of third party performances in United States elections">strongest third party performances</a> in U.S. history. Taft won 23.2 percent of the popular vote but just 8 electoral votes, while Debs won 6 percent of the popular vote. In the concurrent <a href="/wiki/1912_United_States_elections" title="1912 United States elections">congressional elections</a>, Democrats retained control of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">House</a> and won a majority in the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">Senate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-cooper173174_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cooper173174-105">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Wilson's victory made him the first Southerner to win a presidential election since the <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_War" title="American Civil War">Civil War</a>, the first Democratic president since Grover Cleveland left office in 1897,<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> and the first and only president to hold a Ph.D.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Presidency_.281913.E2.80.931921.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidency_(1913–1921)">Presidency (1913–1921)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_Woodrow_Wilson" title="Presidency of Woodrow Wilson">Presidency of Woodrow Wilson</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For a chronological guide, see <a href="/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Woodrow_Wilson_presidency" title="Timeline of the Woodrow Wilson presidency">Timeline of the Woodrow Wilson presidency</a>.</div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Wilson_Cabinet_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Wilson_Cabinet_2.jpg/220px-Wilson_Cabinet_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="123" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Wilson_Cabinet_2.jpg/330px-Wilson_Cabinet_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Wilson_Cabinet_2.jpg/440px-Wilson_Cabinet_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2352" data-file-height="1320" /></a><figcaption>Wilson and his cabinet in 1918</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the election, Wilson chose <a href="/wiki/William_Jennings_Bryan" title="William Jennings Bryan">William Jennings Bryan</a> as Secretary of State, and Bryan offered advice on the remaining members of Wilson's cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/William_Gibbs_McAdoo" title="William Gibbs McAdoo">William Gibbs McAdoo</a>, a prominent Wilson supporter who married Wilson's daughter in 1914, became Secretary of the Treasury, and <a href="/wiki/James_Clark_McReynolds" title="James Clark McReynolds">James Clark McReynolds</a>, who had successfully prosecuted several prominent antitrust cases, was chosen as Attorney General.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Publisher <a href="/wiki/Josephus_Daniels" title="Josephus Daniels">Josephus Daniels</a>, a party loyalist and prominent white supremacist from North Carolina,<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> was chosen to be Secretary of the Navy, while young New York attorney <a href="/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt" title="Franklin D. Roosevelt">Franklin D. Roosevelt</a> became Assistant Secretary of the Navy.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Wilson's chief of staff ("secretary") was <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Patrick_Tumulty" title="Joseph Patrick Tumulty">Joseph Patrick Tumulty</a>, who acted as a political buffer and intermediary with the press.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> The most important foreign policy adviser and confidant was "Colonel" <a href="/wiki/Edward_M._House" title="Edward M. House">Edward M. House</a>; Berg writes that, "in access and influence, [House] outranked everybody in Wilson's Cabinet."<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="New_Freedom_domestic_agenda">New Freedom domestic agenda</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Congress_opening_message_63rd_02406a.tif" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Congress_opening_message_63rd_02406a.tif/lossy-page1-220px-Congress_opening_message_63rd_02406a.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="296" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Congress_opening_message_63rd_02406a.tif/lossy-page1-330px-Congress_opening_message_63rd_02406a.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Congress_opening_message_63rd_02406a.tif/lossy-page1-440px-Congress_opening_message_63rd_02406a.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="7627" data-file-height="10260" /></a><figcaption>Wilson giving his first <a href="/wiki/State_of_the_Union" title="State of the Union">State of the Union</a> address in 1913 before a joint session of <a href="/wiki/United_States_Congress" title="United States Congress">Congress</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> which initiated the modern practice of the State of the Union being given in person before all members of Congress<sup id="cite_ref-AppSou_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AppSou-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Wilson introduced a comprehensive program of domestic legislation at the outset of his administration, something no president had ever done before.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> He announced four major domestic priorities: the <a href="/wiki/Conservation_movement" title="Conservation movement">conservation</a> of natural resources, banking reform, <a href="/wiki/Tariffs_in_United_States_history" class="mw-redirect" title="Tariffs in United States history">tariff</a> reduction, and better access to raw materials for farmers by breaking up Western mining trusts.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> Wilson introduced these proposals in April 1913 in a speech delivered to a joint session of Congress, becoming the first president since <a href="/wiki/John_Adams" title="John Adams">John Adams</a> to address Congress in person.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> Wilson's first two years in office largely focused on his domestic agenda. With trouble with Mexico and the outbreak of World War I in 1914, foreign affairs increasingly dominated his presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Tariff_and_tax_legislation">Tariff and tax legislation</span></h4>
<p>Democrats had long seen high tariff rates as equivalent to unfair taxes on consumers, and tariff reduction was their first priority.<sup id="cite_ref-Clements3637_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clements3637-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> He argued that the system of high tariffs "cuts us off from our proper part in the commerce of the world, violates the just principles of taxation, and makes the government a facile instrument in the hands of private interests."<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> By late May 1913, House Majority Leader <a href="/wiki/Oscar_Underwood" title="Oscar Underwood">Oscar Underwood</a> had passed a bill in the House that cut the average tariff rate by 10 percent and imposed a tax on personal income above $4,000.<sup id="cite_ref-cooper216218_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cooper216218-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> Underwood's bill represented the largest downward revision of the tariff since the Civil War. It aggressively cut rates for raw materials, goods deemed to be "necessities," and products produced domestically by trusts, but it retained higher tariff rates for luxury goods.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nevertheless, the passage of the tariff bill in the Senate was a challenge. Some Southern and Western Democrats wanted the continued protection of their wool and sugar industries, and Democrats had a narrower majority in the upper house.<sup id="cite_ref-Clements3637_120-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clements3637-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Wilson met extensively with Democratic senators and appealed directly to the people through the press. After weeks of hearings and debate, Wilson and Secretary of State Bryan managed to unite Senate Democrats behind the bill.<sup id="cite_ref-cooper216218_122-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cooper216218-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> The Senate voted 44 to 37 in favor of the bill, with only one Democrat voting against it and only one Republican voting for it. Wilson signed the <a href="/wiki/Revenue_Act_of_1913" title="Revenue Act of 1913">Revenue Act of 1913</a> (called the Underwood Tariff) into law on October 3, 1913.<sup id="cite_ref-cooper216218_122-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cooper216218-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> The Revenue Act of 1913 reduced tariffs and replaced the lost revenue with a federal income tax of one percent on incomes above $3,000, affecting the richest three percent of the population.<sup id="cite_ref-weisman230282_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-weisman230282-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> The policies of the Wilson administration had a durable impact on the composition of government revenue, which now primarily came from taxation rather than tariffs.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Federal_Reserve_System">Federal Reserve System</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Federal_Reserve_System" title="History of the Federal Reserve System">History of the Federal Reserve System</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Federal_Reserve_Districts_Map_-_Banks_%26_Branches.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Federal_Reserve_Districts_Map_-_Banks_%26_Branches.png/220px-Federal_Reserve_Districts_Map_-_Banks_%26_Branches.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="140" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Federal_Reserve_Districts_Map_-_Banks_%26_Branches.png/330px-Federal_Reserve_Districts_Map_-_Banks_%26_Branches.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Federal_Reserve_Districts_Map_-_Banks_%26_Branches.png/440px-Federal_Reserve_Districts_Map_-_Banks_%26_Branches.png 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1272" /></a><figcaption>Map of Federal Reserve Districts with Federal Reserve banks (in black circles), District branches (in black squares), and the Federal Reserve's national headquarters in red</figcaption></figure>
<p>Wilson did not wait to complete the Revenue Act of 1913 before proceeding to the next item on his agenda—banking. By the time Wilson took office, countries like Britain and Germany had established government-run <a href="/wiki/Central_bank" title="Central bank">central banks</a>, but the United States had not had a central bank since the <a href="/wiki/Bank_War" title="Bank War">Bank War</a> of the 1830s.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> In the aftermath of the <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1907" title="Panic of 1907">nationwide financial crisis in 1907</a>, there was general agreement to create some sort of central banking system to provide a more elastic currency and to coordinate responses to financial panics. Wilson sought a middle ground between progressives such as Bryan and conservative Republicans like <a href="/wiki/Nelson_Aldrich" class="mw-redirect" title="Nelson Aldrich">Nelson Aldrich</a>, who, as chairman of the <a href="/wiki/National_Monetary_Commission" title="National Monetary Commission">National Monetary Commission</a>, had put forward a plan for a central bank that would give private financial interests a large degree of control over the monetary system.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> Wilson declared that the banking system must be "public not private, [and] must be vested in the government itself so that the banks must be the instruments, not the masters, of business."<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Democrats crafted a compromise plan in which private banks would control twelve regional <a href="/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Bank" title="Federal Reserve Bank">Federal Reserve Banks</a>, but a controlling interest in the system was placed in a central board filled with presidential appointees. Wilson convinced Democrats on the left that the new plan met their demands.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> Finally the Senate voted 54–34 to approve the <a href="/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Act" title="Federal Reserve Act">Federal Reserve Act</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> The new system began operations in 1915, and it played a key role in financing the <a href="/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I" title="Allies of World War I">Allied</a> and American war efforts in World War I.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Antitrust_legislation">Antitrust legislation</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/History_of_United_States_antitrust_law" title="History of United States antitrust law">History of United States antitrust law</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Woodrow_Wilson_Priming_the_Prosperity_Pump,_1914_political_cartoon_by_Berryman.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Woodrow_Wilson_Priming_the_Prosperity_Pump%2C_1914_political_cartoon_by_Berryman.jpg/220px-Woodrow_Wilson_Priming_the_Prosperity_Pump%2C_1914_political_cartoon_by_Berryman.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="270" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Woodrow_Wilson_Priming_the_Prosperity_Pump%2C_1914_political_cartoon_by_Berryman.jpg/330px-Woodrow_Wilson_Priming_the_Prosperity_Pump%2C_1914_political_cartoon_by_Berryman.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Woodrow_Wilson_Priming_the_Prosperity_Pump%2C_1914_political_cartoon_by_Berryman.jpg/440px-Woodrow_Wilson_Priming_the_Prosperity_Pump%2C_1914_political_cartoon_by_Berryman.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1031" data-file-height="1267" /></a><figcaption>A 1913 <a href="/wiki/Clifford_K._Berryman" title="Clifford K. Berryman">Clifford K. Berryman</a> cartoon with Wilson addressing the economy by pumping it full of tariff, currency, and antitrust laws</figcaption></figure>
<p>Having passed major legislation lowering the tariff and reforming the banking structure, Wilson next sought antitrust legislation to enhance the <a href="/wiki/Sherman_Antitrust_Act" title="Sherman Antitrust Act">Sherman Antitrust Act</a> of 1890.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> The Sherman Antitrust Act barred any "contract, combination...or conspiracy, in restraint of trade," but had proved ineffective in preventing the rise of large business combinations known as <a href="/wiki/Trust_(business)" title="Trust (business)">trusts</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> An elite group of businessmen dominated the boards of major banks and railroads, and they used their power to prevent competition by new companies.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> With Wilson's support, Congressman <a href="/wiki/Henry_De_Lamar_Clayton_Jr." title="Henry De Lamar Clayton Jr.">Henry Clayton, Jr.</a> introduced a bill that would ban several anti-competitive practices such as <a href="/wiki/Price_discrimination" title="Price discrimination">discriminatory pricing</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tying_(commerce)" title="Tying (commerce)">tying</a>, <a href="/wiki/Exclusive_dealing" title="Exclusive dealing">exclusive dealing</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Interlocking_directorate" title="Interlocking directorate">interlocking directorates</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As the difficulty of banning all anti-competitive practices via legislation became clear, Wilson came to back legislation that would create a new agency, the <a href="/wiki/Federal_Trade_Commission" title="Federal Trade Commission">Federal Trade Commission</a> (FTC), to investigate antitrust violations and enforce antitrust laws independently of the Justice Department. With bipartisan support, Congress passed the <a href="/wiki/Federal_Trade_Commission_Act_of_1914" title="Federal Trade Commission Act of 1914">Federal Trade Commission Act of 1914</a>, which incorporated Wilson's ideas regarding the FTC.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> One month after signing the Federal Trade Commission Act of 1914, Wilson signed the <a href="/wiki/Clayton_Antitrust_Act_of_1914" title="Clayton Antitrust Act of 1914">Clayton Antitrust Act of 1914</a>, which built on the Sherman Act by defining and banning several anti-competitive practices.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Labor_and_agriculture">Labor and agriculture</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Labor_history_of_the_United_States" title="Labor history of the United States">Labor history of the United States</a></div>
