<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Woodrow_Wilson_(1913).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/President_Woodrow_Wilson_%281913%29.jpg/220px-President_Woodrow_Wilson_%281913%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="274" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/President_Woodrow_Wilson_%281913%29.jpg/330px-President_Woodrow_Wilson_%281913%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/President_Woodrow_Wilson_%281913%29.jpg/440px-President_Woodrow_Wilson_%281913%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2411" data-file-height="3000" /></a><figcaption>Wilson's 1913 official presidential portrait</figcaption></figure>
<p>Wilson thought a child labor law would probably be unconstitutional but reversed himself in 1916 with a close election approaching. In 1916, after intense campaigns by the <a href="/wiki/National_Child_Labor_Committee" title="National Child Labor Committee">National Child Labor Committee</a> (NCLC) and the <a href="/wiki/National_Consumers_League" title="National Consumers League">National Consumers League</a>, the Congress passed the <a href="/wiki/Keating%E2%80%93Owen_Act" title="Keating–Owen Act">Keating–Owen Act</a>, making it illegal to ship goods in interstate commerce if they were made in factories employing children under specified ages. Southern Democrats were opposed but did not filibuster. Wilson endorsed the bill at the last minute under pressure from party leaders who stressed how popular the idea was, especially among the emerging class of women voters. He told Democratic Congressmen they needed to pass this law and also a workman's compensation law to satisfy the national progressive movement and to win the 1916 election against a reunited GOP. It was the first federal child labor law. However, the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States" title="Supreme Court of the United States">U.S. Supreme Court</a> struck down the law in <i><a href="/wiki/Hammer_v._Dagenhart" title="Hammer v. Dagenhart">Hammer v. Dagenhart</a></i> (1918). Congress then passed a law taxing businesses that used child labor, but that was struck down by the Supreme Court in <i><a href="/wiki/Bailey_v._Drexel_Furniture_Co." title="Bailey v. Drexel Furniture Co.">Bailey v. Drexel Furniture</a></i> (1923). Child labor was finally ended in the 1930s.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> He approved the goal of upgrading the harsh working conditions for merchant sailors and signed LaFollette's <a href="/wiki/Seamen%27s_Act" title="Seamen&#39;s Act">Seamen's Act</a> of 1915.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson called on the Labor Department to mediate conflicts between labor and management. In 1914, Wilson dispatched soldiers to help bring an end to the <a href="/wiki/Colorado_Coalfield_War" title="Colorado Coalfield War">Colorado Coalfield War</a>, one of the deadliest labor disputes in American history.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> In 1916 he pushed Congress to enact the <a href="/wiki/Eight-hour_day" title="Eight-hour day">eight-hour work day</a> for railroad workers, which ended a major strike. It was "the boldest intervention in labor relations that any president had yet attempted."<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson disliked the excessive government involvement in the <a href="/wiki/Federal_Farm_Loan_Act" title="Federal Farm Loan Act">Federal Farm Loan Act</a>, which created twelve regional banks empowered to provide low-interest loans to farmers. Nevertheless, he needed the farm vote to survive the upcoming 1916 election, so he signed it.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Territories_and_immigration">Territories and immigration</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/History_of_immigration_to_the_United_States" title="History of immigration to the United States">History of immigration to the United States</a></div>
<p>Wilson embraced the long-standing Democratic policy against owning colonies, and he worked for the gradual autonomy and ultimate independence of the <a href="/wiki/Philippines" title="Philippines">Philippines</a>, which had been acquired in 1898. The <a href="/wiki/Moro_Rebellion" title="Moro Rebellion">final phases</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Philippine%E2%80%93American_War" title="Philippine–American War">Philippine–American War</a> were still ongoing during the first several months of Wilson's presidency, with the American victory at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Bud_Bagsak" title="Battle of Bud Bagsak">Battle of Bud Bagsak</a> in June 1913 bringing a final end to anti-American resistance on the islands. Continuing the policy of his predecessors, Wilson increased self-governance on the islands by granting <a href="/wiki/Filipinos" title="Filipinos">Filipinos</a> greater control over the Philippine Legislature. The <a href="/wiki/Jones_Act_of_1916" class="mw-redirect" title="Jones Act of 1916">Jones Act of 1916</a> committed the United States to the eventual independence of the Philippines, and granted Filipinos further autonomy with the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Philippine_Senate" class="mw-redirect" title="Philippine Senate">Philippine Senate</a>, replacing the American-run <a href="/wiki/Philippine_Commission" title="Philippine Commission">Philippine Commission</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> In 1916, Wilson <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_the_Danish_West_Indies" title="Treaty of the Danish West Indies">purchased by treaty</a> the <a href="/wiki/Danish_West_Indies" title="Danish West Indies">Danish West Indies</a>, renamed as the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Virgin_Islands" title="United States Virgin Islands">United States Virgin Islands</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Immigration from Europe declined significantly once World War I began and Wilson paid little attention to the issue during his presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> However, he looked favorably upon the "new immigrants" from southern and eastern Europe, and twice vetoed laws passed by Congress intended to restrict their entry, though the later veto was overridden.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Judicial_appointments">Judicial appointments</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_Supreme_Court_candidates" title="Woodrow Wilson Supreme Court candidates">Woodrow Wilson Supreme Court candidates</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For a more comprehensive list, see <a href="/wiki/List_of_federal_judges_appointed_by_Woodrow_Wilson" title="List of federal judges appointed by Woodrow Wilson">List of federal judges appointed by Woodrow Wilson</a>.</div>
<p>Wilson nominated three men to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Supreme Court">United States Supreme Court</a>, all of whom were confirmed by the U.S. Senate. In 1914, Wilson nominated sitting Attorney General <a href="/wiki/James_Clark_McReynolds" title="James Clark McReynolds">James Clark McReynolds</a>. Despite his credentials as an ardent trust buster,<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> McReynolds became a staple of the court's conservative bloc until his retirement in 1941.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> According to Berg, Wilson considered appointing McReynolds one of his biggest mistakes in office.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> In 1916, Wilson <a href="/wiki/Louis_Brandeis_Supreme_Court_nomination" title="Louis Brandeis Supreme Court nomination">nominated Louis Brandeis to the Court</a>, setting off a major debate in the Senate over Brandeis's progressive ideology and his religion; Brandeis was the first <a href="/wiki/Jewish" class="mw-redirect" title="Jewish">Jewish</a> nominee to the Supreme Court. Ultimately, Wilson was able to convince Senate Democrats to vote to confirm Brandeis who served on the court until 1939. In contrast to McReynolds, Brandeis became one of the court's leading progressive voices.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> When a second vacancy arose in 1916, Wilson appointed progressive lawyer <a href="/wiki/John_Hessin_Clarke" title="John Hessin Clarke">John Hessin Clarke</a>. Clarke was confirmed by the Senate and served on the Court until retiring in 1922.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First-term_foreign_policy">First-term foreign policy</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_Woodrow_Wilson_administration" title="Foreign policy of the Woodrow Wilson administration">Foreign policy of the Woodrow Wilson administration</a></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Latin_America">Latin America</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/United_States_involvement_in_the_Mexican_Revolution" title="United States involvement in the Mexican Revolution">United States involvement in the Mexican Revolution</a> and <a href="/wiki/Banana_Wars" title="Banana Wars">Banana Wars</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:VillaUncleSamBerrymanCartoon.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/VillaUncleSamBerrymanCartoon.png/220px-VillaUncleSamBerrymanCartoon.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="266" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/VillaUncleSamBerrymanCartoon.png/330px-VillaUncleSamBerrymanCartoon.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/VillaUncleSamBerrymanCartoon.png/440px-VillaUncleSamBerrymanCartoon.png 2x" data-file-width="4298" data-file-height="5202" /></a><figcaption>A cartoon depicting <a href="/wiki/Uncle_Sam" title="Uncle Sam">Uncle Sam</a> entering <a href="/wiki/Mexico" title="Mexico">Mexico</a> in 1916 to punish Pancho Villa with Uncle Sam saying, "I've had about enough of this."</figcaption></figure>
<p>Wilson sought to move away from the foreign policy of his predecessors, which he viewed as imperialistic, and he rejected Taft's <a href="/wiki/Dollar_Diplomacy" class="mw-redirect" title="Dollar Diplomacy">Dollar Diplomacy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, he frequently intervened in <a href="/wiki/Latin_America" title="Latin America">Latin America</a>, saying in 1913, "I am going to teach the <a href="/wiki/South_America" title="South America">South American</a> republics to elect good men."<sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> The 1914 <a href="/wiki/Bryan%E2%80%93Chamorro_Treaty" title="Bryan–Chamorro Treaty">Bryan–Chamorro Treaty</a> converted <a href="/wiki/Nicaragua" title="Nicaragua">Nicaragua</a> into a de facto protectorate, and the U.S. <a href="/wiki/United_States_occupation_of_Nicaragua" title="United States occupation of Nicaragua">stationed soldiers</a> there throughout Wilson's presidency. The Wilson administration <a href="/wiki/United_States_occupation_of_the_Dominican_Republic_(1916%E2%80%9324)" class="mw-redirect" title="United States occupation of the Dominican Republic (1916–24)">sent troops to occupy</a> the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Republic" title="Dominican Republic">Dominican Republic</a> and <a href="/wiki/United_States_occupation_of_Haiti" title="United States occupation of Haiti">intervene</a> in <a href="/wiki/Haiti" title="Haiti">Haiti</a>, and Wilson also authorized military interventions in <a href="/wiki/Cuba" title="Cuba">Cuba</a>, <a href="/wiki/Panama" title="Panama">Panama</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Honduras" title="Honduras">Honduras</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson took office during the <a href="/wiki/Mexican_Revolution" title="Mexican Revolution">Mexican Revolution</a>, which had begun in 1911 after liberals overthrew the military dictatorship of <a href="/wiki/Porfirio_D%C3%ADaz" title="Porfirio Díaz">Porfirio Díaz</a>. Shortly before Wilson took office, conservatives retook power through a coup led by <a href="/wiki/Victoriano_Huerta" title="Victoriano Huerta">Victoriano Huerta</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> Wilson rejected the legitimacy of Huerta's "government of butchers" and demanded Mexico hold democratic elections.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> After Huerta arrested U.S. Navy personnel who had accidentally landed in a restricted zone near the northern port town of <a href="/wiki/Tampico" title="Tampico">Tampico</a>, Wilson <a href="/wiki/United_States_occupation_of_Veracruz" title="United States occupation of Veracruz">dispatched the Navy to occupy</a> the Mexican city of <a href="/wiki/Veracruz" title="Veracruz">Veracruz</a>. A strong backlash against the American intervention among Mexicans of all political affiliations convinced Wilson to abandon his plans to expand the U.S. military intervention, but the intervention nonetheless helped convince Huerta to flee from the country.<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> A group led by <a href="/wiki/Venustiano_Carranza" title="Venustiano Carranza">Venustiano Carranza</a> established control over a significant proportion of Mexico, and Wilson recognized Carranza's government in October 1915.<sup id="cite_ref-Clements_(1992),_pp._99–100_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clements_(1992),_pp._99–100-158">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Carranza continued to face various opponents within Mexico, including <a href="/wiki/Pancho_Villa" title="Pancho Villa">Pancho Villa</a>, whom Wilson had earlier described as "a sort of Robin Hood."<sup id="cite_ref-Clements_(1992),_pp._99–100_158-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clements_(1992),_pp._99–100-158">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> In early 1916, Pancho Villa raided the village of <a href="/wiki/Columbus,_New_Mexico" title="Columbus, New Mexico">Columbus, New Mexico</a>, killing or wounding dozens of Americans and causing an enormous nationwide American demand for his punishment. Wilson ordered General <a href="/wiki/John_J._Pershing" title="John J. Pershing">John J. Pershing</a> and 4,000 troops across the border to capture Villa. By April, Pershing's forces had broken up and dispersed Villa's bands, but Villa remained on the loose and Pershing continued his pursuit deep into Mexico. Carranza then pivoted against the Americans and accused them of a punitive invasion, leading to several incidents that nearly led to war. Tensions subsided after Mexico agreed to release several American prisoners, and bilateral negotiations began under the auspices of the Mexican-American Joint High Commission. Eager to withdraw from Mexico due to tensions in Europe, Wilson ordered Pershing to withdraw, and the last American soldiers left in February 1917.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Neutrality_in_World_War_I">Neutrality in World War I</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_American_War-Dog_by_Oscar_Cesare_1916.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/The_American_War-Dog_by_Oscar_Cesare_1916.jpg/220px-The_American_War-Dog_by_Oscar_Cesare_1916.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="331" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/The_American_War-Dog_by_Oscar_Cesare_1916.jpg/330px-The_American_War-Dog_by_Oscar_Cesare_1916.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/The_American_War-Dog_by_Oscar_Cesare_1916.jpg/440px-The_American_War-Dog_by_Oscar_Cesare_1916.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3560" data-file-height="5350" /></a><figcaption>A cartoon of Wilson and "Jingo", the American war dog, ridiculing jingoes baying for war</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> broke out in July 1914, pitting the <a href="/wiki/Central_Powers" title="Central Powers">Central Powers</a> (Germany, <a href="/wiki/Austria-Hungary" title="Austria-Hungary">Austria-Hungary</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a>, and later <a href="/wiki/Bulgaria" title="Bulgaria">Bulgaria</a>) against the <a href="/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I" title="Allies of World War I">Allied Powers</a> (Britain, <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a>, <a href="/wiki/Russia" title="Russia">Russia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Serbia" title="Serbia">Serbia</a>, and several other countries). The war fell into a long stalemate with very high casualties on the <a href="/wiki/Western_Front_(World_War_I)" title="Western Front (World War I)">Western Front</a> in France. Both sides rejected offers by Wilson and House to mediate an end the conflict.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> From 1914 until early 1917, Wilson's primary foreign policy objectives were to keep the United States out of the war in Europe and to broker a peace agreement.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> He insisted that all U.S. government actions be neutral, stating that Americans "must be impartial in thought as well as in action, must put a curb upon our sentiments as well as upon every transaction that might be construed as a preference of one party to the struggle before another."<sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> As a neutral power, the U.S. insisted on its right to trade with both sides. However the powerful British Royal Navy imposed a <a href="/wiki/Blockade_of_Germany_(1914%E2%80%931919)" title="Blockade of Germany (1914–1919)">blockade of Germany</a>. To appease Washington, London agreed to continue purchasing certain major American commodities such as cotton at pre-war prices, and in the event an American merchant vessel was caught with contraband, the Royal Navy was under orders to buy the entire cargo and release the vessel.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Wilson passively accepted this situation.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In response to the British blockade, Germany launched a <a href="/wiki/U-boat_Campaign_(World_War_I)" class="mw-redirect" title="U-boat Campaign (World War I)">submarine campaign</a> against merchant vessels in the seas surrounding the British Isles.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> In early 1915, the Germans sank three American ships; Wilson took the view, based on some reasonable evidence, that these incidents were accidental, and a settlement of claims could be postponed until the end of the war.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> In May 1915, a German submarine torpedoed the British ocean liner <a href="/wiki/RMS_Lusitania" title="RMS Lusitania">RMS <i>Lusitania</i></a>, killing 1,198 passengers, including 128 American citizens.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> Wilson publicly responded by saying, "there is such a thing as a man being too proud to fight. There is such a thing as a nation being so right that it does not need to convince others by force that it is right".<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Wilson demanded that the German government "take immediate steps to prevent the recurrence" of incidents like the sinking of the <i>Lusitania</i>. In response, Bryan, who believed that Wilson had placed the defense of American trade rights above neutrality, resigned from the Cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> In March 1916, the <a href="/wiki/SS_Sussex" title="SS Sussex">SS <i>Sussex</i></a>, an unarmed ferry under the French flag, was torpedoed in the English Channel and four Americans were counted among the dead. Wilson extracted from Germany a pledge to constrain submarine warfare to the rules of cruiser warfare, which represented a major diplomatic concession.<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Interventionists, led by Theodore Roosevelt, wanted war with Germany and attacked Wilson's refusal to build up the army in anticipation of war.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> After the sinking of the <i>Lusitania</i> and the resignation of Bryan, Wilson publicly committed himself to what became known as the "<a href="/wiki/Preparedness_Movement" title="Preparedness Movement">preparedness movement</a>", and began to build up the army and the navy.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> In June 1916, Congress passed the <a href="/wiki/National_Defense_Act_of_1916" title="National Defense Act of 1916">National Defense Act of 1916</a>, which established the <a href="/wiki/Reserve_Officers%27_Training_Corps" title="Reserve Officers&#39; Training Corps">Reserve Officers' Training Corps</a> and expanded the <a href="/wiki/United_States_National_Guard" class="mw-redirect" title="United States National Guard">National Guard</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> Later in the year, Congress passed the <a href="/wiki/Naval_Act_of_1916" title="Naval Act of 1916">Naval Act of 1916</a>, which provided for a major expansion of the navy.<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Remarriage">Remarriage</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:(left_to_right)_Margaret_Wilson,_Mrs._Woodrow_Wilson,_Jessie_Woodrow_Wilson,_Woodrow_Wilson,_Eleanor_Randolph_Wilson_(1912).png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/%28left_to_right%29_Margaret_Wilson%2C_Mrs._Woodrow_Wilson%2C_Jessie_Woodrow_Wilson%2C_Woodrow_Wilson%2C_Eleanor_Randolph_Wilson_%281912%29.png/220px-%28left_to_right%29_Margaret_Wilson%2C_Mrs._Woodrow_Wilson%2C_Jessie_Woodrow_Wilson%2C_Woodrow_Wilson%2C_Eleanor_Randolph_Wilson_%281912%29.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="213" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/8a/%28left_to_right%29_Margaret_Wilson%2C_Mrs._Woodrow_Wilson%2C_Jessie_Woodrow_Wilson%2C_Woodrow_Wilson%2C_Eleanor_Randolph_Wilson_%281912%29.png 1.5x" data-file-width="284" data-file-height="275" /></a><figcaption>The Wilson family in 1912</figcaption></figure>
<p>The health of Ellen Wilson declined after her husband entered office, and doctors diagnosed her with <a href="/wiki/Bright%27s_disease" title="Bright&#39;s disease">Bright's disease</a> in July 1914.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> She died on August 6, 1914.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> President Wilson was deeply affected by the loss, falling into depression.<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> On March 18, 1915, Wilson met <a href="/wiki/Edith_Bolling_Galt" class="mw-redirect" title="Edith Bolling Galt">Edith Bolling Galt</a> at a White House tea.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> Galt was a widow and jeweler who was also from the South. After several meetings, Wilson fell in love with her, and he proposed marriage to her in May 1915. Galt initially rebuffed him, but Wilson was undeterred and continued the courtship.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> Edith gradually warmed to the relationship, and they became engaged in September 1915.<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> They were married on December 18, 1915. Woodrow Wilson joined <a href="/wiki/John_Tyler" title="John Tyler">John Tyler</a> and Grover Cleveland as the only presidents to marry while in office.<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidential_election_of_1916">Presidential election of 1916</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1916_United_States_presidential_election" title="1916 United States presidential election">1916 United States presidential election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Woodrow_Wilson,_accepting_Democratic_nomination,_1916.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Woodrow_Wilson%2C_accepting_Democratic_nomination%2C_1916.JPG/220px-Woodrow_Wilson%2C_accepting_Democratic_nomination%2C_1916.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Woodrow_Wilson%2C_accepting_Democratic_nomination%2C_1916.JPG/330px-Woodrow_Wilson%2C_accepting_Democratic_nomination%2C_1916.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Woodrow_Wilson%2C_accepting_Democratic_nomination%2C_1916.JPG/440px-Woodrow_Wilson%2C_accepting_Democratic_nomination%2C_1916.JPG 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="595" /></a><figcaption>Wilson accepts the Democratic Party nomination for president in 1916</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1916.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/ElectoralCollege1916.svg/220px-ElectoralCollege1916.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="128" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/ElectoralCollege1916.svg/330px-ElectoralCollege1916.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/ElectoralCollege1916.svg/440px-ElectoralCollege1916.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1020" data-file-height="593" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/1916_United_States_presidential_election" title="1916 United States presidential election">1916 electoral college map</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Wilson was renominated at the <a href="/wiki/1916_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1916 Democratic National Convention">1916 Democratic National Convention</a> without opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> In an effort to win progressive voters, Wilson called for legislation providing for an eight-hour day and six-day workweek, health and safety measures, the prohibition of child labor, and safeguards for female workers. He also favored a minimum wage for all work performed by and for the federal government.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> The Democrats also campaigned on the slogan "He Kept Us Out of War," and warned that a Republican victory would mean war with Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> Hoping to reunify the progressive and conservative wings of the party, the <a href="/wiki/1916_Republican_National_Convention" title="1916 Republican National Convention">1916 Republican National Convention</a> nominated Supreme Court Justice <a href="/wiki/Charles_Evans_Hughes" title="Charles Evans Hughes">Charles Evans Hughes</a> for president; as a justice he had been totally out of politics in 1912. Though Republicans attacked Wilson's foreign policy on various grounds, domestic affairs generally dominated the campaign. Republicans campaigned against Wilson's New Freedom policies, especially tariff reduction, the new income taxes, and the <a href="/wiki/Adamson_Act" title="Adamson Act">Adamson Act</a>, which they derided as "class legislation."<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The election was close and the outcome was in doubt with Hughes ahead in the East, and Wilson in the South and West. The decision came down to California. On November 10, California certified that Wilson had won the state by 3,806 votes, giving him a majority of the electoral vote. Nationally, Wilson won 277 electoral votes and 49.2 percent of the popular vote, while Hughes won 254 electoral votes and 46.1 percent of the popular vote.<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> Wilson was able to win by picking up many votes that had gone to Roosevelt or Debs in 1912.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> He swept the <a href="/wiki/Solid_South" title="Solid South">Solid South</a> and won all but a handful of Western states, while Hughes won most of the Northeastern and Midwestern states.<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> Wilson's re-election made him the first Democrat since <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a> (in 1832) to win two consecutive terms. The Democrats kept control of Congress.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Entering_the_war">Entering the war</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/American_entry_into_World_War_I" title="American entry into World War I">American entry into World War I</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/United_States_in_World_War_I" title="United States in World War I">United States in World War I</a> and <a href="/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_Woodrow_Wilson_administration" title="Foreign policy of the Woodrow Wilson administration">Foreign policy of the Woodrow Wilson administration</a></div>
<p>In January 1917, the Germans initiated a new policy of <a href="/wiki/Unrestricted_submarine_warfare" title="Unrestricted submarine warfare">unrestricted submarine warfare</a> against ships in the seas around the British Isles. German leaders knew that the policy would likely provoke U.S. entrance into the war, but they hoped to defeat the Allied Powers before the U.S. could fully mobilize.<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> In late February, the U.S. public learned of the <a href="/wiki/Zimmermann_Telegram" title="Zimmermann Telegram">Zimmermann Telegram</a>, a secret diplomatic communication in which Germany sought to convince Mexico to join it in a war against the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> After a series of attacks on American ships, Wilson held a Cabinet meeting on March 20; all Cabinet members agreed that the time had come for the United States to enter the war.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> The Cabinet members believed that Germany was engaged in a commercial war against the United States, and that the United States had to respond with a formal declaration of war.<sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On April 2, 1917, Wilson asked Congress for a declaration of war against Germany, arguing that Germany was engaged in "nothing less than war against the government and people of the United States." He requested a military draft to raise the army, increased taxes to pay for military expenses, loans to Allied governments, and increased industrial and agricultural production.<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> He stated, "we have no selfish ends to serve. We desire no conquest, no dominion... no material compensation for the sacrifices we shall freely make. We are but one of the champions of the rights of mankind. We shall be satisfied when those rights have been made as secure as the faith and freedom of the nations can make them."<sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Declaration_of_war_by_the_United_States" title="Declaration of war by the United States">declaration of war by the United States</a> <a href="/wiki/1917_United_States_declaration_of_war_on_Germany" class="mw-redirect" title="1917 United States declaration of war on Germany">against Germany</a> passed Congress with strong bipartisan majorities on April 6, 1917.<sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> The United States later <a href="/wiki/United_States_declaration_of_war_on_Austria-Hungary" title="United States declaration of war on Austria-Hungary">declared war</a> against Austria-Hungary in December 1917.<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With the U.S. entrance into the war, Wilson and Secretary of War <a href="/wiki/Newton_D._Baker" title="Newton D. Baker">Newton D. Baker</a> launched an expansion of the army, with the goal of creating a 300,000-member <a href="/wiki/Regular_Army_(United_States)" title="Regular Army (United States)">Regular Army</a>, a 440,000-member <a href="/wiki/Army_National_Guard" title="Army National Guard">National Guard</a>, and a 500,000-member conscripted force known as the "<a href="/wiki/Army_of_the_United_States" title="Army of the United States">National Army</a>." Despite some resistance to conscription and to the commitment of American soldiers abroad, large majorities of both houses of Congress voted to impose conscription with the <a href="/wiki/Selective_Service_Act_of_1917" title="Selective Service Act of 1917">Selective Service Act of 1917</a>. Seeking to avoid the draft riots of the Civil War, the bill established local draft boards that were charged with determining who should be drafted. By the end of the war, nearly 3&#160;million men had been drafted.<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> The navy also saw tremendous expansion, and Allied shipping losses dropped substantially due to U.S. contributions and a new emphasis on the <a href="/wiki/Convoys_in_World_War_I" title="Convoys in World War I">convoy system</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:World_1914_empires_colonies_territory.PNG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/World_1914_empires_colonies_territory.PNG/510px-World_1914_empires_colonies_territory.PNG" decoding="async" width="510" height="210" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/World_1914_empires_colonies_territory.PNG/765px-World_1914_empires_colonies_territory.PNG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/World_1914_empires_colonies_territory.PNG/1020px-World_1914_empires_colonies_territory.PNG 2x" data-file-width="1522" data-file-height="628" /></a><figcaption>Map of the <a href="/wiki/Great_power" title="Great power">great powers</a> and their empires in 1914</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Fourteen_Points">The Fourteen Points</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Fourteen_Points" title="Fourteen Points">Fourteen Points</a></div>
<p>Wilson sought the establishment of "an organized common peace" that would help prevent future conflicts. In this goal, he was opposed not just by the Central Powers, but also the other Allied Powers, who, to various degrees, sought to win concessions and to impose a punitive peace agreement on the Central Powers.<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> On January 8, 1918, Wilson delivered a speech, known as the Fourteen Points, wherein he articulated his administration's long term war objectives. Wilson called for the establishment of an association of nations to guarantee the independence and territorial integrity of all nations—a <a href="/wiki/League_of_Nations" title="League of Nations">League of Nations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> Other points included the evacuation of occupied territory, the establishment of an independent <a href="/wiki/Poland" title="Poland">Poland</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Self-determination" title="Self-determination">self-determination</a> for the peoples of Austria-Hungary and the Ottoman Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Course_of_the_war">Course of the war</span></h4>
<p>Under the command of General Pershing, the <a href="/wiki/American_Expeditionary_Forces" title="American Expeditionary Forces">American Expeditionary Forces</a> first arrived in France in mid-1917.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> Wilson and Pershing rejected the British and French proposal that American soldiers integrate into existing Allied units, giving the United States more freedom of action but requiring for the creation of new organizations and supply chains.<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> Russia exited the war after signing the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Brest-Litovsk" title="Treaty of Brest-Litovsk">Treaty of Brest-Litovsk</a> in March 1918, allowing Germany to shift soldiers from the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Front_(World_War_I)" title="Eastern Front (World War I)">Eastern Front</a> of the war.<sup id="cite_ref-clements149151_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-clements149151-205">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> Hoping to break Allied lines before American soldiers could arrive in full force, the Germans launched the <a href="/wiki/German_spring_offensive" title="German spring offensive">Spring Offensive</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Western_Front_(World_War_I)" title="Western Front (World War I)">Western Front</a>. Both sides suffered hundreds of thousands of casualties as the Germans forced back the British and French, but Germany was unable to capture the French capital of <a href="/wiki/Paris" title="Paris">Paris</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> There were only 175,000 American soldiers in Europe at the end of 1917, but by mid-1918 10,000 Americans were arriving in Europe per day.<sup id="cite_ref-clements149151_205-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-clements149151-205">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> With American forces having joined in the fight, the Allies defeated Germany in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Belleau_Wood" title="Battle of Belleau Wood">Battle of Belleau Wood</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Ch%C3%A2teau-Thierry_(1918)" title="Battle of Château-Thierry (1918)">Battle of Château-Thierry</a>. Beginning in August, the Allies launched the <a href="/wiki/Hundred_Days_Offensive" title="Hundred Days Offensive">Hundred Days Offensive</a>, pushing back the exhausted German army.<sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> Meanwhile, French and British leaders convinced Wilson to send a few thousand American soldiers to join the <a href="/wiki/Allied_intervention_in_the_Russian_Civil_War" title="Allied intervention in the Russian Civil War">Allied intervention</a> in Russia, which was in the midst of a <a href="/wiki/Russian_Civil_War" title="Russian Civil War">civil war</a> between the Communist <a href="/wiki/Bolsheviks" title="Bolsheviks">Bolsheviks</a> and the <a href="/wiki/White_movement" title="White movement">White movement</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By the end of September 1918, the German leadership no longer believed it could win the war, and Kaiser <a href="/wiki/Wilhelm_II,_German_Emperor" title="Wilhelm II, German Emperor">Wilhelm II</a> appointed a new government led by <a href="/wiki/Prince_Maximilian_of_Baden" title="Prince Maximilian of Baden">Prince Maximilian of Baden</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> Baden immediately sought an armistice with Wilson, with the Fourteen Points to serve as the basis of the German surrender.<sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> House procured agreement to the armistice from France and Britain, but only after threatening to conclude a unilateral armistice without them.<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> Germany and the Allied Powers brought an end to the fighting with the signing of the <a href="/wiki/Armistice_of_11_November_1918" title="Armistice of 11 November 1918">Armistice of 11 November 1918</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> Austria-Hungary had signed the <a href="/wiki/Armistice_of_Villa_Giusti" title="Armistice of Villa Giusti">Armistice of Villa Giusti</a> eight days earlier, while the Ottoman Empire had signed the <a href="/wiki/Armistice_of_Mudros" title="Armistice of Mudros">Armistice of Mudros</a> in October. By the end of the war, 116,000 American servicemen had died, and another 200,000 had been wounded.<sup id="cite_ref-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-213">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Home_front">Home front</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/United_States_home_front_during_World_War_I" title="United States home front during World War I">United States home front during World War I</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:FoodWillWinTheGreatWarNOLA.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/FoodWillWinTheGreatWarNOLA.jpg/220px-FoodWillWinTheGreatWarNOLA.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="171" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/FoodWillWinTheGreatWarNOLA.jpg/330px-FoodWillWinTheGreatWarNOLA.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/FoodWillWinTheGreatWarNOLA.jpg/440px-FoodWillWinTheGreatWarNOLA.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2677" data-file-height="2080" /></a><figcaption>A banner reading, "Food will win the war—don't waste it", in front of city hall in <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans" title="New Orleans">New Orleans</a> in October 1918</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Women_workers_in_ordnance_shops,_Midvale_Steel_and_Ordnance_Company,_Nicetown,_Pennsylvania._Hand_chipping_with_pneumati_-_NARA_-_530774.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Women_workers_in_ordnance_shops%2C_Midvale_Steel_and_Ordnance_Company%2C_Nicetown%2C_Pennsylvania._Hand_chipping_with_pneumati_-_NARA_-_530774.jpg/220px-Women_workers_in_ordnance_shops%2C_Midvale_Steel_and_Ordnance_Company%2C_Nicetown%2C_Pennsylvania._Hand_chipping_with_pneumati_-_NARA_-_530774.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="156" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Women_workers_in_ordnance_shops%2C_Midvale_Steel_and_Ordnance_Company%2C_Nicetown%2C_Pennsylvania._Hand_chipping_with_pneumati_-_NARA_-_530774.jpg/330px-Women_workers_in_ordnance_shops%2C_Midvale_Steel_and_Ordnance_Company%2C_Nicetown%2C_Pennsylvania._Hand_chipping_with_pneumati_-_NARA_-_530774.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Women_workers_in_ordnance_shops%2C_Midvale_Steel_and_Ordnance_Company%2C_Nicetown%2C_Pennsylvania._Hand_chipping_with_pneumati_-_NARA_-_530774.jpg/440px-Women_workers_in_ordnance_shops%2C_Midvale_Steel_and_Ordnance_Company%2C_Nicetown%2C_Pennsylvania._Hand_chipping_with_pneumati_-_NARA_-_530774.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2928" data-file-height="2070" /></a><figcaption>Women workers in an ordnance shop in <a href="/wiki/Pennsylvania" title="Pennsylvania">Pennsylvania</a> in 1918</figcaption></figure>
<p>With the American entrance into <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> in April 1917, Wilson became a war-time president. The <a href="/wiki/War_Industries_Board" title="War Industries Board">War Industries Board</a>, headed by <a href="/wiki/Bernard_Baruch" title="Bernard Baruch">Bernard Baruch</a>, was established to set U.S. war manufacturing policies and goals. Future President <a href="/wiki/Herbert_Hoover" title="Herbert Hoover">Herbert Hoover</a> led the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Food_Administration" title="United States Food Administration">Food Administration</a>; the <a href="/wiki/Federal_Fuel_Administration" class="mw-redirect" title="Federal Fuel Administration">Federal Fuel Administration</a>, run by <a href="/wiki/Harry_Augustus_Garfield" title="Harry Augustus Garfield">Harry Augustus Garfield</a>, introduced <a href="/wiki/Daylight_saving_time" title="Daylight saving time">daylight saving time</a> and rationed fuel supplies; William McAdoo was in charge of war bond efforts; <a href="/wiki/Vance_C._McCormick" title="Vance C. McCormick">Vance C. McCormick</a> headed the War Trade Board. These men, known collectively as the "war cabinet", met weekly with Wilson.<sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> Because he was heavily focused on foreign policy during World War I, Wilson delegated a large degree of authority over the home front to his subordinates.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> In the midst of the war, the federal budget soared from $1&#160;billion in <a href="/wiki/Fiscal_year" title="Fiscal year">fiscal year</a> 1916 to $19&#160;billion in fiscal year 1919.<sup id="cite_ref-clements156157_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-clements156157-216">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> In addition to spending on its own military build-up, Wall Street in 1914–1916 and the Treasury in 1917–1918 provided large loans to the Allied countries, thus financing the war effort of Britain and France.<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Seeking to avoid the high levels of inflation that had accompanied the heavy borrowing of the <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_War" title="American Civil War">American Civil War</a>, the Wilson administration raised taxes during the war.<sup id="cite_ref-218" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-218">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/War_Revenue_Act_of_1917" title="War Revenue Act of 1917">War Revenue Act of 1917</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Revenue_Act_of_1918" title="Revenue Act of 1918">Revenue Act of 1918</a> raised the top tax rate to 77 percent, greatly increased the number of Americans paying the income tax, and levied an <a href="/wiki/Excess_profits_tax" title="Excess profits tax">excess profits tax</a> on businesses and individuals.<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> Despite these tax acts, the United States was forced to borrow heavily to finance the war effort. Treasury Secretary McAdoo authorized the issuing of low-interest war bonds and, to attract investors, made interest on the bonds tax-free. The bonds proved so popular among investors that many borrowed money in order to buy more bonds. The purchase of bonds, along with other war-time pressures, resulted in rising inflation, though this inflation was partly matched by rising wages and profits.<sup id="cite_ref-clements156157_216-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-clements156157-216">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>To shape public opinion, Wilson in 1917 established the first modern propaganda office, the <a href="/wiki/Committee_on_Public_Information" title="Committee on Public Information">Committee on Public Information</a> (CPI), headed by <a href="/wiki/George_Creel" title="George Creel">George Creel</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-220" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-220">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson called on voters in <a href="/wiki/1918_United_States_elections" title="1918 United States elections">the 1918 off-year elections</a> to elect Democrats as an endorsement of his policies. However the Republicans won over alienated <a href="/wiki/German-Americans" class="mw-redirect" title="German-Americans">German-Americans</a> and took control.<sup id="cite_ref-221" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-221">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> Wilson refused to coordinate or compromise with the new leaders of House and Senate—Senator <a href="/wiki/Henry_Cabot_Lodge" title="Henry Cabot Lodge">Henry Cabot Lodge</a> became his nemesis.<sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 1919, Wilson's Attorney General, <a href="/wiki/A._Mitchell_Palmer" title="A. Mitchell Palmer">A. Mitchell Palmer</a>, began to target anarchists, <a href="/wiki/Industrial_Workers_of_the_World" title="Industrial Workers of the World">Industrial Workers of the World</a> members, and other antiwar groups in what became known as the <a href="/wiki/Palmer_Raids" title="Palmer Raids">Palmer Raids</a>. Thousands were arrested for incitement to violence, espionage, or sedition. Wilson by that point was incapacitated and was not told what was happening.<sup id="cite_ref-cooper201209_223-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cooper201209-223">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Aftermath_of_World_War_I">Aftermath of World War I</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_Woodrow_Wilson_administration" title="Foreign policy of the Woodrow Wilson administration">Foreign policy of the Woodrow Wilson administration</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Map_Europe_1923-en.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Map_Europe_1923-en.svg/220px-Map_Europe_1923-en.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="149" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Map_Europe_1923-en.svg/330px-Map_Europe_1923-en.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Map_Europe_1923-en.svg/440px-Map_Europe_1923-en.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1037" data-file-height="704" /></a><figcaption>Several new European states were established at the <a href="/wiki/Paris_Peace_Conference" class="mw-redirect" title="Paris Peace Conference">Paris Peace Conference</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Paris_Peace_Conference">Paris Peace Conference</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Aftermath_of_World_War_I" title="Aftermath of World War I">Aftermath of World War I</a> and <a href="/wiki/Paris_Peace_Conference_(1919%E2%80%931920)" title="Paris Peace Conference (1919–1920)">Paris Peace Conference (1919–1920)</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Big_four.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Big_four.jpg/220px-Big_four.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="169" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Big_four.jpg/330px-Big_four.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Big_four.jpg/440px-Big_four.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2616" data-file-height="2008" /></a><figcaption>The "Big Four" at the <a href="/wiki/Paris_Peace_Conference" class="mw-redirect" title="Paris Peace Conference">Paris Peace Conference</a> on May 27, 1919, following the end of <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> with Wilson standing next to <a href="/wiki/Georges_Clemenceau" title="Georges Clemenceau">Georges Clemenceau</a> on the right</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Review_of_reviews_and_world%27s_work_(1890)_(14586729460).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Review_of_reviews_and_world%27s_work_%281890%29_%2814586729460%29.jpg/220px-Review_of_reviews_and_world%27s_work_%281890%29_%2814586729460%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="153" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Review_of_reviews_and_world%27s_work_%281890%29_%2814586729460%29.jpg/330px-Review_of_reviews_and_world%27s_work_%281890%29_%2814586729460%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Review_of_reviews_and_world%27s_work_%281890%29_%2814586729460%29.jpg/440px-Review_of_reviews_and_world%27s_work_%281890%29_%2814586729460%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3872" data-file-height="2698" /></a><figcaption>Vast throngs of Italians in <a href="/wiki/Milan" title="Milan">Milan</a> gather to welcome Wilson</figcaption></figure> 
<p>After the signing of the armistice, Wilson traveled to Europe to lead the American delegation to the Paris Peace Conference, thereby becoming the first incumbent president to travel to Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> Although Republicans now controlled Congress, Wilson shut them out. Senate Republicans and even some Senate Democrats complained about their lack of representation in the delegation. It consisted of Wilson, Colonel House,<sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Robert_Lansing" title="Robert Lansing">Robert Lansing</a>, General <a href="/wiki/Tasker_H._Bliss" title="Tasker H. Bliss">Tasker H. Bliss</a>, and diplomat <a href="/wiki/Henry_White_(diplomat)" title="Henry White (diplomat)">Henry White</a> was the only Republican, and he was not an active partisan.<sup id="cite_ref-227" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-227">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> Save for a two-week return to the United States, Wilson remained in Europe for six months, where he focused on reaching a peace treaty to formally end the war. Wilson, British Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/David_Lloyd_George" title="David Lloyd George">David Lloyd George</a>, French Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Georges_Clemenceau" title="Georges Clemenceau">Georges Clemenceau</a>, and Italian Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Vittorio_Emanuele_Orlando" title="Vittorio Emanuele Orlando">Vittorio Emanuele Orlando</a> made up the "<a href="/wiki/The_Big_Four_(World_War_I)" class="mw-redirect" title="The Big Four (World War I)">Big Four</a>", the Allied leaders with the most influence at the Paris Peace Conference.<sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> Wilson had an illness during the conference, and some experts believe the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_flu" title="Spanish flu">Spanish flu</a> was the cause.<sup id="cite_ref-spanish_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-spanish-229">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Unlike other Allied leaders, Wilson did not seek territorial gains or material concessions from the Central Powers. His chief goal was the establishment of the League of Nations, which he saw as the "keystone of the whole programme".<sup id="cite_ref-230" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-230">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> Wilson himself presided over the committee that drafted the <a href="/wiki/Covenant_of_the_League_of_Nations" title="Covenant of the League of Nations">Covenant of the League of Nations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> The covenant bound members to respect <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_religion" title="Freedom of religion">freedom of religion</a>, treat racial minorities fairly, and peacefully settle disputes through organizations like the <a href="/wiki/Permanent_Court_of_International_Justice" title="Permanent Court of International Justice">Permanent Court of International Justice</a>. Article X of the League Covenant required all nations to defend League members against external aggression.<sup id="cite_ref-232" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-232">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> Japan proposed that the conference endorse a <a href="/wiki/Racial_Equality_Proposal,_1919" class="mw-redirect" title="Racial Equality Proposal, 1919">racial equality clause</a>; Wilson was indifferent to the issue, but acceded to strong opposition from Australia and Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-233" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-233">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> The Covenant of the League of Nations was incorporated into the conference's <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles" title="Treaty of Versailles">Treaty of Versailles</a>, which ended the war with Germany, and into other peace treaties.<sup id="cite_ref-234" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-234">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Aside from the establishment the League of Nations and solidifying a lasting world peace, Wilson's other main goal at the Paris Peace Conference was that self-determination be the primary basis used for drawing new international borders.<sup id="cite_ref-Berg_2013,_pp._534,_563_235-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Berg_2013,_pp._534,_563-235">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup> However, in pursuit of his League of Nations, Wilson conceded several points to the other powers present at the conference. Germany was required to permanently cede territory, pay war reparations, relinquish all of her overseas colonies and dependencies and submit to <a href="/wiki/Allied_occupation_of_the_Rhineland" class="mw-redirect" title="Allied occupation of the Rhineland">military occupation in the Rhineland</a>. Additionally, a <a href="/wiki/Article_231_of_the_Treaty_of_Versailles" title="Article 231 of the Treaty of Versailles">clause</a> in the treaty specifically named Germany as responsible for the war. Wilson agreed to allowing the Allied European powers and Japan to essentially expand their empires by establishing de facto colonies in the Middle East, Africa, and Asia out the former German and Ottoman Empires; these territorial awards to the victorious countries were thinly disguised as "<a href="/wiki/League_of_Nations_mandates" class="mw-redirect" title="League of Nations mandates">League of Nations mandates</a>". The Japanese acquisition of German interests in the <a href="/wiki/Shandong_Peninsula" title="Shandong Peninsula">Shandong Peninsula</a> of China proved especially <a href="/wiki/Shandong_Problem" title="Shandong Problem">unpopular</a>, as it undercut Wilson's promise of self-government. Wilson's hopes for achieving self-determination saw some success when the conference recognized multiple new and independent states created in Eastern Europe, including <a href="/wiki/Principality_of_Albania" title="Principality of Albania">Albania</a>, <a href="/wiki/First_Czechoslovak_Republic" title="First Czechoslovak Republic">Czechoslovakia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Second_Polish_Republic" title="Second Polish Republic">Poland</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Yugoslavia" title="Kingdom of Yugoslavia">Yugoslavia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Berg_2013,_pp._534,_563_235-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Berg_2013,_pp._534,_563-235">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-236" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-236">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-237" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-237">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The conference finished negotiations in May 1919, at which point the new leaders of <a href="/wiki/Republican_Germany" class="mw-redirect" title="Republican Germany">republican Germany</a> viewed the treaty for the first time. Some German leaders favored repudiating the peace due to the harshness of the terms, though ultimately Germany signed the treaty on June 28, 1919.<sup id="cite_ref-238" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-238">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> Wilson was unable to convince the other Allied powers, France in particular, to temper the harshness of the settlement being leveled at the defeated Central Powers, especially Germany.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> For his efforts towards creating a lasting world peace, Wilson was awarded the 1919 <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize" title="Nobel Peace Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-239" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-239">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Ratification_debate_and_defeat">Ratification debate and defeat</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Woodrow_Wilson_-_NH_18.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/President_Woodrow_Wilson_-_NH_18.jpeg/170px-President_Woodrow_Wilson_-_NH_18.jpeg" decoding="async" width="170" height="140" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/President_Woodrow_Wilson_-_NH_18.jpeg/255px-President_Woodrow_Wilson_-_NH_18.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/President_Woodrow_Wilson_-_NH_18.jpeg/340px-President_Woodrow_Wilson_-_NH_18.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="5435" data-file-height="4474" /></a><figcaption>Wilson returning from the <a href="/wiki/Versailles_Peace_Conference" class="mw-redirect" title="Versailles Peace Conference">Versailles Peace Conference</a> on <a href="/wiki/USS_George_Washington" title="USS George Washington">USS <i>George Washington</i></a>, as she steamed up <a href="/wiki/New_York_Harbor" title="New York Harbor">New York Harbor</a> on July 8, 1919; the <a href="/wiki/Weimar_National_Assembly" title="Weimar National Assembly">Weimar National Assembly</a> in Germany formally ratified the treaty the following day in a vote of 209 to 116<sup id="cite_ref-pinson_240-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pinson-240">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Ratification of the Treaty of Versailles required the support of two-thirds of the Senate, a difficult proposition given that Republicans held a narrow majority in the Senate after the <a href="/wiki/1918_U.S._elections" class="mw-redirect" title="1918 U.S. elections">1918 U.S. elections</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Clements_(1992),_pp._190–191_241-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clements_(1992),_pp._190–191-241">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup> Republicans were outraged by Wilson's failure to discuss the war or its aftermath with them, and an intensely partisan battle developed in the Senate. Republican Senator <a href="/wiki/Henry_Cabot_Lodge" title="Henry Cabot Lodge">Henry Cabot Lodge</a> supported a version of the treaty that required Wilson to compromise. Wilson refused.<sup id="cite_ref-Clements_(1992),_pp._190–191_241-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clements_(1992),_pp._190–191-241">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup> Some Republicans, including former President Taft and former Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Elihu_Root" title="Elihu Root">Elihu Root</a>, favored ratification of the treaty with some modifications, and their public support gave Wilson some chance of winning the treaty's ratification.<sup id="cite_ref-Clements_(1992),_pp._190–191_241-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clements_(1992),_pp._190–191-241">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The debate over the treaty centered around a debate over the American role in the world community in the post-war era, and senators fell into three main groups. The first group, consisting of most Democrats, favored the treaty.<sup id="cite_ref-Clements_(1992),_pp._190–191_241-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clements_(1992),_pp._190–191-241">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup> Fourteen senators, mostly Republicans, were known as the "<a href="/wiki/Irreconcilables" title="Irreconcilables">irreconcilables</a>" as they completely opposed U.S. entrance into the League of Nations. Some of these irreconcilables opposed the treaty for its failure to emphasize decolonization and disarmament, while others feared surrendering American freedom of action to an international organization.<sup id="cite_ref-herring427430_242-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-herring427430-242">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> The remaining group of senators, known as "reservationists", accepted the idea of the League but sought varying degrees of change to ensure the protection of American sovereignty and the right of Congress to decide on going to war.<sup id="cite_ref-herring427430_242-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-herring427430-242">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Article X of the League Covenant, which sought to create a system of <a href="/wiki/Collective_security" title="Collective security">collective security</a> by requiring League members to protect one another against external aggression, seemed to force the U.S. to join in any war the League decided upon.<sup id="cite_ref-243" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-243">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup> Wilson consistently refused to compromise, partly due to concerns about having to re-open negotiations with the other treaty signatories.<sup id="cite_ref-244" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-244">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup> When Lodge was on the verge of building a two-thirds majority to ratify the Treaty with ten reservations, Wilson forced his supporters to vote Nay on March 19, 1920, thereby closing the issue. Cooper says that "nearly every League advocate" went along with Lodge, but their efforts "failed solely because Wilson admittedly rejected all reservations proposed in the Senate."<sup id="cite_ref-245" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-245">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Thomas_A._Bailey" title="Thomas A. Bailey">Thomas A. Bailey</a> calls Wilson's action "the supreme act of infanticide".<sup id="cite_ref-246" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-246">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup> He adds: "The treaty was slain in the house of its friends rather than in the house of its enemies. In the final analysis it was not the two-thirds rule, or the 'irreconcilables,' or Lodge, or the 'strong' and 'mild' reservationists, but Wilson and his docile following who delivered the fatal stab."<sup id="cite_ref-247" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-247">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Health_collapses">Health collapses</span></h4>
<p>To bolster public support for ratification, Wilson barnstormed the Western states, but he returned to the White House in late September due to health problems.<sup id="cite_ref-248" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-248">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup> On October 2, 1919, Wilson suffered a serious stroke, leaving him paralyzed on his left side, and with only partial vision in the right eye.<sup id="cite_ref-249" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-249">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-250" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-250">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup> He was confined to bed for weeks and sequestered from everyone except his wife and his physician, <a href="/wiki/Cary_Grayson" class="mw-redirect" title="Cary Grayson">Cary Grayson</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup> Bert E. Park, a neurosurgeon who examined Wilson's medical records after his death, writes that Wilson's illness affected his personality in various ways, making him prone to "disorders of emotion, impaired impulse control, and defective judgment."<sup id="cite_ref-252" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-252">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup> Anxious to help the president recover, Tumulty, Grayson, and the First Lady determined what documents the president read and who was allowed to communicate with him. For her influence in the administration, some have described Edith Wilson as "the first female President of the United States."<sup id="cite_ref-253" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-253">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup> Link states that by November 1919, Wilson's "recovery was only partial at best. His mind remained relatively clear; but he was physically enfeebled, and the disease had wrecked his emotional constitution and aggravated all his more unfortunate personal traits.<sup id="cite_ref-254" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-254">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Throughout late 1919, Wilson's inner circle concealed the severity of his health issues.<sup id="cite_ref-255" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-255">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> By February 1920, the president's true condition was publicly known. Many expressed qualms about Wilson's fitness for the presidency at a time when the League fight was reaching a climax, and domestic issues such as strikes, unemployment, inflation and the threat of Communism were ablaze. In mid-March 1920, Lodge and his Republicans formed a coalition with the pro-treaty Democrats to pass a treaty with reservations, but Wilson rejected this compromise and enough Democrats followed his lead to defeat ratification.<sup id="cite_ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> No one close to Wilson was willing to certify, as required by the Constitution, his "inability to discharge the powers and duties of the said office."<sup id="cite_ref-257" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-257">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup> Though some members of Congress encouraged Vice President Marshall to assert his claim to the presidency, Marshall never attempted to replace Wilson.<sup id="cite_ref-marshallsen_258-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-marshallsen-258">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup> Wilson's lengthy period of incapacity while serving as president was nearly unprecedented; of the previous presidents, only <a href="/wiki/James_Garfield" class="mw-redirect" title="James Garfield">James Garfield</a> had been in a similar situation, but Garfield retained greater control of his mental faculties and faced relatively few pressing issues.<sup id="cite_ref-259" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-259">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Demobilization">Demobilization</span></h4>
<p>When the war ended the Wilson Administration dismantled the wartime boards and regulatory agencies.<sup id="cite_ref-260" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-260">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup> Demobilization was chaotic and at times violent; four million soldiers were sent home with little money and few benefits. In 1919, strikes in major industries broke out, disrupting the economy.<sup id="cite_ref-261" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-261">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup> The country experienced further turbulence as a <a href="/wiki/Red_Summer_of_1919" class="mw-redirect" title="Red Summer of 1919">series of race riots</a> broke out in the summer of 1919.<sup id="cite_ref-262" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-262">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup> In 1920, the economy plunged into a <a href="/wiki/Depression_of_1920%E2%80%9321" class="mw-redirect" title="Depression of 1920–21">severe economic depression</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-263" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-263">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup> unemployment rose to 12 percent, and the price of agricultural products sharply declined.<sup id="cite_ref-264" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-264">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Red_Scare_and_Palmer_Raids">Red Scare and Palmer Raids</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:June_3_1919_Newspapers_of_the_1919_United_States_anarchist_bombings.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/June_3_1919_Newspapers_of_the_1919_United_States_anarchist_bombings.png/220px-June_3_1919_Newspapers_of_the_1919_United_States_anarchist_bombings.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="301" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/June_3_1919_Newspapers_of_the_1919_United_States_anarchist_bombings.png/330px-June_3_1919_Newspapers_of_the_1919_United_States_anarchist_bombings.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/June_3_1919_Newspapers_of_the_1919_United_States_anarchist_bombings.png/440px-June_3_1919_Newspapers_of_the_1919_United_States_anarchist_bombings.png 2x" data-file-width="1142" data-file-height="1562" /></a><figcaption>Newspaper headlines on June 3, 1919, covering the <a href="/wiki/1919_United_States_anarchist_bombings" title="1919 United States anarchist bombings">bombings</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Following the <a href="/wiki/October_Revolution" title="October Revolution">Bolshevik Revolution</a> in <a href="/wiki/Russia" title="Russia">Russia</a> and similar revolutionary attempts in <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hungary" title="Hungary">Hungary</a>, many Americans feared the possibility of terrorism in the United States. Such concerns were inflamed by the <a href="/wiki/1919_United_States_anarchist_bombings" title="1919 United States anarchist bombings">bombings</a> in April 1919 when anarchists mailed 38 bombs to prominent Americans; one person was killed but most packages were intercepted. Nine more mail bombs were sent in June; injuring several people.<sup id="cite_ref-265" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-265">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup> Fresh fears combined with a patriotic national mood sparking the "<a href="/wiki/First_Red_Scare" title="First Red Scare">First Red Scare</a>" in 1919. Attorney General Palmer from November 1919 to January 1920 launched the <a href="/wiki/Palmer_Raids" title="Palmer Raids">Palmer Raids</a> to suppress radical organizations. Over 10,000 people were arrested and 556 aliens were deported, including <a href="/wiki/Emma_Goldman" title="Emma Goldman">Emma Goldman</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-266" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-266">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> Palmer's activities met resistance from the courts and some senior administration officials. No one told Wilson what Palmer was doing.<sup id="cite_ref-267" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-267">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-268" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-268">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup> Later in 1920 the <a href="/wiki/Wall_Street_bombing" title="Wall Street bombing">Wall Street bombing</a> on September 16, killed 40 and injured hundreds in the deadliest terrorist attack on American soil up to that point. Anarchists took credit and promised more violence to come; they escaped capture.<sup id="cite_ref-bgage1_269-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bgage1-269">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Prohibition_and_women.27s_suffrage"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prohibition_and_women's_suffrage">Prohibition and women's suffrage</span></h4>
<p><a href="/wiki/Prohibition_in_the_United_States" title="Prohibition in the United States">Prohibition</a> developed as an unstoppable reform during <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, but the <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_Woodrow_Wilson" title="Presidency of Woodrow Wilson">Wilson administration</a> played only a minor role.<sup id="cite_ref-270" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-270">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Eighteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Eighteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution">Eighteenth Amendment</a> passed Congress and was ratified by the states in 1919. In October 1919, Wilson vetoed the <a href="/wiki/Volstead_Act" title="Volstead Act">Volstead Act</a>, legislation designed to enforce Prohibition, but his veto was overridden by Congress.<sup id="cite_ref-271" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-271">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-272" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-272">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson opposed <a href="/wiki/Women%27s_suffrage_in_the_United_States" title="Women&#39;s suffrage in the United States">women's suffrage</a> in 1911 because he believed women lacked the public experience needed to be good voters. The actual evidence of how women voters behaved in the western states changed his mind, and he came to feel they could indeed be good voters. He did not speak publicly on the issue except to echo the Democratic Party position that suffrage was a state matter, primarily because of strong opposition in the white South to black voting rights.<sup id="cite_ref-273" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-273">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup>
