</p><p>In a 1918 speech before Congress, Wilson for the first time backed a national right to vote: "We have made partners of the women in this war....Shall we admit them only to a partnership of suffering and sacrifice and toil and not to a partnership of privilege and right?"<sup id="cite_ref-WWWSM_274-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WWWSM-274">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup> The House passed a constitutional amendment providing for women's suffrage nationwide, but this stalled in the Senate. Wilson continually pressured the Senate to vote for the amendment, telling senators that its ratification was vital to winning the war.<sup id="cite_ref-275" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-275">&#91;273&#93;</a></sup> The Senate finally approved it in June 1919, and the requisite number of states ratified the <a href="/wiki/Nineteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Nineteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution">Nineteenth Amendment</a> in August 1920.<sup id="cite_ref-276" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-276">&#91;274&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="1920_election">1920 election</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/1920_United_States_presidential_election" title="1920 United States presidential election">1920 United States presidential election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1920.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/ElectoralCollege1920.svg/220px-ElectoralCollege1920.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="128" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/ElectoralCollege1920.svg/330px-ElectoralCollege1920.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/ElectoralCollege1920.svg/440px-ElectoralCollege1920.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1020" data-file-height="593" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States)" title="Republican Party (United States)">Republican</a> presidential nominee <a href="/wiki/Warren_G._Harding" title="Warren G. Harding">Warren G. Harding</a> defeated Democratic nominee James Cox in the <a href="/wiki/1920_United_States_presidential_election" title="1920 United States presidential election">1920 United States presidential election</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Despite his medical incapacity, Wilson wanted to run for a third term. While the <a href="/wiki/1920_Democratic_National_Convention" title="1920 Democratic National Convention">1920 Democratic National Convention</a> strongly endorsed Wilson's policies, Democratic leaders refused, nominating instead a ticket consisting of Governor <a href="/wiki/James_M._Cox" title="James M. Cox">James M. Cox</a> and Assistant Secretary of the Navy <a href="/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt" title="Franklin D. Roosevelt">Franklin D. Roosevelt</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-cooper565569_277-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cooper565569-277">&#91;275&#93;</a></sup> The Republicans centered their campaign around opposition to Wilson's policies, with Senator <a href="/wiki/Warren_G._Harding" title="Warren G. Harding">Warren G. Harding</a> promising a "<a href="/wiki/Return_to_normalcy" title="Return to normalcy">return to normalcy</a>". Wilson largely stayed out of the campaign, although he endorsed Cox and continued to advocate for U.S. membership in the League of Nations. Harding won the election in a landslide, capturing over 60% of the popular vote and winning every state <a href="/wiki/Solid_South" title="Solid South">outside of the South</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-278" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-278">&#91;276&#93;</a></sup> Wilson met with Harding for tea on his last day in office, March 3, 1921. Due to his health, Wilson was unable to attend <a href="/wiki/Inauguration_of_Warren_G._Harding" title="Inauguration of Warren G. Harding">the inauguration</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-279" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-279">&#91;277&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On December 10, 1920, Wilson was awarded the 1919 <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize" title="Nobel Peace Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</a> "for his role as founder of the League of Nations".<sup id="cite_ref-280" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-280">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-281" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-281">&#91;279&#93;</a></sup> Wilson became the <a href="/wiki/List_of_heads_of_state_and_government_Nobel_laureates" title="List of heads of state and government Nobel laureates">second sitting United States president</a> after <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt" title="Theodore Roosevelt">Theodore Roosevelt</a> to become a <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Laureates" class="mw-redirect" title="Nobel Peace Laureates">Nobel Peace Laureate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-282" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-282">&#91;280&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Final_years_and_death_.281921.E2.80.931924.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_years_and_death_(1921–1924)">Final years and death (1921–1924)</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Woodrow_Wilson_tomb_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Woodrow_Wilson_tomb_2.jpg/220px-Woodrow_Wilson_tomb_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="174" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Woodrow_Wilson_tomb_2.jpg/330px-Woodrow_Wilson_tomb_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Woodrow_Wilson_tomb_2.jpg/440px-Woodrow_Wilson_tomb_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2556" data-file-height="2022" /></a><figcaption>The final resting place of Woodrow Wilson at <a href="/wiki/Washington_National_Cathedral" title="Washington National Cathedral">Washington National Cathedral</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>After the end of his second term in 1921, Wilson and his wife moved from the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a> to a townhouse in the <a href="/wiki/Kalorama_(Washington,_D.C.)" title="Kalorama (Washington, D.C.)">Kalorama</a> section of <a href="/wiki/Washington,_D.C." title="Washington, D.C.">Washington, D.C.</a><sup id="cite_ref-283" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-283">&#91;281&#93;</a></sup> He continued to follow politics as President Harding and the Republican Congress repudiated membership in the League of Nations, cut taxes, and raised tariffs.<sup id="cite_ref-284" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-284">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup> In 1921, Wilson opened a law practice with former Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Bainbridge_Colby" title="Bainbridge Colby">Bainbridge Colby</a>. Wilson showed up the first day but never returned, and the practice was closed by the end of 1922. Wilson tried writing, and he produced a few short essays after enormous effort; they "marked a sad finish to a formerly great literary career."<sup id="cite_ref-285" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-285">&#91;283&#93;</a></sup> He declined to write memoirs, but frequently met with <a href="/wiki/Ray_Stannard_Baker" title="Ray Stannard Baker">Ray Stannard Baker</a>, who wrote a three-volume biography of Wilson that was published in 1922.<sup id="cite_ref-286" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-286">&#91;284&#93;</a></sup> In August 1923, Wilson attended the funeral of his successor, Warren Harding.<sup id="cite_ref-cooper581590_287-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cooper581590-287">&#91;285&#93;</a></sup> On November 10, 1923, Wilson made his last national address, delivering a short <a href="/wiki/Armistice_Day" title="Armistice Day">Armistice Day</a> radio speech from the library of his home.<sup id="cite_ref-288" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-288">&#91;286&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-289" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-289">&#91;287&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><span class="anchor" id="Death"></span>
</p><p>Wilson's health did not markedly improve after leaving office,<sup id="cite_ref-290" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-290">&#91;288&#93;</a></sup> declining rapidly in January 1924. Woodrow Wilson died on February 3, 1924, at the age of 67.<sup id="cite_ref-291" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-291">&#91;289&#93;</a></sup> He was interred in <a href="/wiki/Washington_National_Cathedral" title="Washington National Cathedral">Washington National Cathedral</a>, being the only president whose final resting place lies within the nation's capital.<sup id="cite_ref-292" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-292">&#91;290&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Race_relations">Race relations</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_and_race" title="Woodrow Wilson and race">Woodrow Wilson and race</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Wilson-quote-in-birth-of-a-nation.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="&quot;The white men were roused by a mere instinct of self-preservation ... until at last there had sprung into existence a great Ku Klux Klan, a veritable empire of the South, to protect the Southern country.&quot;" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Wilson-quote-in-birth-of-a-nation.jpg/220px-Wilson-quote-in-birth-of-a-nation.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Wilson-quote-in-birth-of-a-nation.jpg/330px-Wilson-quote-in-birth-of-a-nation.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Wilson-quote-in-birth-of-a-nation.jpg/440px-Wilson-quote-in-birth-of-a-nation.jpg 2x" data-file-width="960" data-file-height="720" /></a><figcaption>Quotation from Woodrow Wilson's <i>History of the American People</i> as reproduced in the film <i><a href="/wiki/The_Birth_of_a_Nation" title="The Birth of a Nation">The Birth of a Nation</a></i></figcaption></figure>
<p>Wilson was born and raised in the <a href="/wiki/Southern_United_States" title="Southern United States">U.S. South</a> by parents who were committed supporters of both slavery and the <a href="/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America" title="Confederate States of America">Confederacy</a>. Academically, Wilson was an apologist for slavery and the <a href="/wiki/Redeemers" title="Redeemers">Redeemers</a>, and one of the foremost promoters of the <a href="/wiki/Lost_Cause" class="mw-redirect" title="Lost Cause">Lost Cause</a> mythology.<sup id="cite_ref-293" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-293">&#91;291&#93;</a></sup> 
Wilson was the first Southerner elected president since <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor" title="Zachary Taylor">Zachary Taylor</a> in <a href="/wiki/1848_United_States_presidential_election" title="1848 United States presidential election">1848</a> and the only former subject of the Confederacy. Wilson's election was celebrated by <a href="/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States" title="Racial segregation in the United States">southern segregationists</a>. At Princeton, Wilson actively dissuaded the admission of African-Americans as students.<sup id="cite_ref-294" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-294">&#91;292&#93;</a></sup> Several historians have spotlighted consistent examples in the public record of Wilson's overtly racist policies and the inclusion of segregationists in his Cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-Foner_295-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Foner-295">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-turner-sadler_296-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-turner-sadler-296">&#91;294&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-JNH_Wolgemuth_297-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JNH_Wolgemuth-297">&#91;295&#93;</a></sup> Other sources say Wilson defended segregation as "a rational, scientific policy" in private and describe him as a man who "loved to tell racist 'darky' jokes about black Americans."<sup id="cite_ref-feagin_298-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-feagin-298">&#91;296&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Gerstle_p103_299-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gerstle_p103-299">&#91;297&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During Wilson's presidency, <a href="/wiki/D._W._Griffith" title="D. W. Griffith">D. W. Griffith</a>'s pro-<a href="/wiki/Ku_Klux_Klan" title="Ku Klux Klan">Ku Klux Klan</a> film <i><a href="/wiki/The_Birth_of_a_Nation" title="The Birth of a Nation">The Birth of a Nation</a></i> (1915) was the first motion picture to be screened in the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-300" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-300">&#91;298&#93;</a></sup> Though he was not initially critical of the movie, Wilson distanced himself from it as public backlash mounted and eventually released a statement condemning the film's message while denying he had been aware of it prior to the screening.<sup id="cite_ref-301" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-301">&#91;299&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-302" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-302">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Segregating_the_federal_bureaucracy">Segregating the federal bureaucracy</span></h3>
<p>By the 1910s, <a href="/wiki/African_American" class="mw-redirect" title="African American">African Americans</a> had become effectively shut out of elected office. Obtaining an executive appointment to a position within the federal bureaucracy was usually the only option for African-American statesmen. According to Berg, Wilson continued to appoint African-Americans to positions that had traditionally been filled by black people, overcoming opposition from many southern senators. <a href="/wiki/Oswald_Garrison_Villard" title="Oswald Garrison Villard">Oswald Garrison Villard</a>, who later became an opponent of his, initially thought that Wilson was not a bigot and supported progress for black people, and he was frustrated by southern opposition in the Senate, to which Wilson capitulated. In a conversation with Wilson, journalist John Palmer Gavit came to the realization that opposition to those views "would certainly precipitate a conflict which would put a complete stop to any legislative program."<sup id="cite_ref-303" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-303">&#91;301&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-304" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-304">&#91;302&#93;</a></sup> Since the end of Reconstruction, both parties recognized certain appointments as unofficially reserved for qualified African-Americans. Wilson appointed a total of nine African-Americans to prominent positions in the federal bureaucracy, eight of whom were Republican carry-overs. For comparison, <a href="/wiki/William_Howard_Taft" title="William Howard Taft">William Howard Taft</a> was met with disdain and outrage from Republicans of both races for appointing thirty-one black officeholders, a record low for a Republican president. Upon taking office, Wilson fired all but two of the seventeen black supervisors in the federal bureaucracy appointed by Taft.<sup id="cite_ref-305" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-305">&#91;303&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-306" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-306">&#91;304&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Since 1863, the U.S. mission to Haiti and Santo Domingo was almost always led by an African-American diplomat regardless of what party the sitting president belonged to; Wilson ended this half-century-old tradition but continued to appoint black diplomats like <a href="/wiki/George_Washington_Buckner" title="George Washington Buckner">George Washington Buckner</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-307" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-307">&#91;305&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-308" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-308">&#91;306&#93;</a></sup> as well as <a href="/wiki/Joseph_L._Johnson" title="Joseph L. Johnson">Joseph L. Johnson</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-poli_johnson_309-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-poli_johnson-309">&#91;307&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-diplomat_johnson_310-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-diplomat_johnson-310">&#91;308&#93;</a></sup> to head the mission to <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-311" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-311">&#91;309&#93;</a></sup> Since the end of Reconstruction, the federal bureaucracy had been possibly the only career path where African-Americans could experience some measure of equality,<sup id="cite_ref-312" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-312">&#91;310&#93;</a></sup> and was the life blood and foundation of the black middle-class.<sup id="cite_ref-313" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-313">&#91;311&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilson's administration escalated the discriminatory hiring policies and segregation of government offices that had begun under <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt" title="Theodore Roosevelt">Theodore Roosevelt</a> and continued under Taft.<sup id="cite_ref-314" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-314">&#91;312&#93;</a></sup> In Wilson's first month in office, Postmaster General <a href="/wiki/Albert_S._Burleson" title="Albert S. Burleson">Albert S. Burleson</a> urged the president to establish segregated government offices.<sup id="cite_ref-wolgemuth_315-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wolgemuth-315">&#91;313&#93;</a></sup> Wilson did not adopt Burleson's proposal but allowed Cabinet Secretaries discretion to segregate their respective departments.<sup id="cite_ref-316" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-316">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup> By the end of 1913, many departments, including the Navy, Treasury, and Post Office, had segregated work spaces, restrooms, and cafeterias.<sup id="cite_ref-wolgemuth_315-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wolgemuth-315">&#91;313&#93;</a></sup> Many agencies used segregation as a pretext to adopt a whites-only employment policy, claiming they lacked facilities for black workers. In these instances, African-Americans employed prior to the Wilson administration were either offered early retirement, transferred, or simply fired.<sup id="cite_ref-317" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-317">&#91;315&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Racial discrimination in federal hiring increased further when after 1914, the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Civil_Service_Commission" title="United States Civil Service Commission">United States Civil Service Commission</a> instituted a new policy requiring job applicants to submit a personal photo with their application.<sup id="cite_ref-318" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-318">&#91;316&#93;</a></sup> As a federal enclave, Washington, D.C., had long offered African-Americans greater opportunities for employment and less glaring discrimination. In 1919, black veterans returning home to D.C. were shocked to discover <a href="/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws" title="Jim Crow laws">Jim Crow laws</a> had set in, many could not go back to the jobs they held prior to the war or even enter the same building they used to work in due to the color of their skin. <a href="/wiki/Booker_T._Washington" title="Booker T. Washington">Booker T. Washington</a> described the situation: "I had never seen the colored people so discouraged and bitter as they are at the present time."<sup id="cite_ref-319" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-319">&#91;317&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="African_Americans_in_the_armed_forces">African Americans in the armed forces</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States_Armed_Forces" title="Racial segregation in the United States Armed Forces">Racial segregation in the United States Armed Forces</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:King,_Stoddard_WW1_draft_card.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/King%2C_Stoddard_WW1_draft_card.jpg/220px-King%2C_Stoddard_WW1_draft_card.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="107" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/King%2C_Stoddard_WW1_draft_card.jpg/330px-King%2C_Stoddard_WW1_draft_card.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/King%2C_Stoddard_WW1_draft_card.jpg/440px-King%2C_Stoddard_WW1_draft_card.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2528" data-file-height="1224" /></a><figcaption>A <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> draft card. The lower left corner could be removed for men of African descent to help keep the military segregated</figcaption></figure>
<p>While segregation had been present in the Army prior to Wilson, its severity increased significantly under his election. During Wilson's first term, the Army and Navy refused to commission new black officers.<sup id="cite_ref-320" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-320">&#91;318&#93;</a></sup> Black officers already serving experienced increased discrimination and were often forced out or discharged on dubious grounds.<sup id="cite_ref-321" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-321">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup> Following the entry of the U.S. into World War I, the War Department drafted hundreds of thousands of black people into the Army, and draftees were paid equally regardless of race. Commissioning of African-Americans officers resumed but units remained segregated and most all-black units were led by white officers.<sup id="cite_ref-322" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-322">&#91;320&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (August 2022)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Unlike the Army, the U.S. Navy was never formally segregated. Following Wilson's appointment of <a href="/wiki/Josephus_Daniels" title="Josephus Daniels">Josephus Daniels</a> as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_the_Navy" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of the Navy">Secretary of the Navy</a>, a system of Jim Crow was swiftly implemented; with ships, training facilities, restrooms, and cafeterias all becoming segregated.<sup id="cite_ref-wolgemuth_315-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wolgemuth-315">&#91;313&#93;</a></sup> While Daniels significantly expanded opportunities for advancement and training available to white sailors, by the time the U.S. entered World War I, African-American sailors had been relegated almost entirely to mess and custodial duties, often assigned to act as servants for white officers.<sup id="cite_ref-323" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-323">&#91;321&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Response_to_racial_violence">Response to racial violence</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:East_St_Louis_Massacre_cartoon,_Morris.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/East_St_Louis_Massacre_cartoon%2C_Morris.jpg/220px-East_St_Louis_Massacre_cartoon%2C_Morris.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="274" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/East_St_Louis_Massacre_cartoon%2C_Morris.jpg/330px-East_St_Louis_Massacre_cartoon%2C_Morris.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/East_St_Louis_Massacre_cartoon%2C_Morris.jpg/440px-East_St_Louis_Massacre_cartoon%2C_Morris.jpg 2x" data-file-width="487" data-file-height="607" /></a><figcaption>A 1917 political cartoon published in <i><a href="/wiki/New_York_Evening_Mail" title="New York Evening Mail">New York Evening Mail</a></i> about the <a href="/wiki/East_St._Louis_riots" class="mw-redirect" title="East St. Louis riots">East St. Louis riots</a> in 1917 with the caption reading, "Mr. President, why not make America safe for democracy?"</figcaption></figure>
<p>In response to the demand for industrial labor, the <a href="/wiki/Great_Migration_(African_American)" title="Great Migration (African American)">Great Migration</a> of African Americans out of the South surged in 1917 and 1918. This migration sparked <a href="/wiki/Mass_racial_violence_in_the_United_States" title="Mass racial violence in the United States">race riots</a>, including the <a href="/wiki/East_St._Louis_riots" class="mw-redirect" title="East St. Louis riots">East St. Louis riots</a> of 1917. In response to these riots, but only after much public outcry, Wilson asked Attorney General <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Watt_Gregory" title="Thomas Watt Gregory">Thomas Watt Gregory</a> if the federal government could intervene to "check these disgraceful outrages". On the advice of Gregory, Wilson did not take direct action against the riots.<sup id="cite_ref-324" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-324">&#91;322&#93;</a></sup> In 1918, Wilson spoke out against <a href="/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States" title="Lynching in the United States">lynching in the United States</a>, stating: "I say plainly that every American who takes part in the action of mob or gives it any sort of continence is no true son of this great democracy but its betrayer, and ... [discredits] her by that single disloyalty to her standards of law and of rights."<sup id="cite_ref-325" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-325">&#91;323&#93;</a></sup> In 1919, another <a href="/wiki/Red_Summer_of_1919" class="mw-redirect" title="Red Summer of 1919">series of race riots</a> occurred in <a href="/wiki/Chicago_Race_Riot_of_1919" class="mw-redirect" title="Chicago Race Riot of 1919">Chicago</a>, <a href="/wiki/Omaha_Race_Riot_of_1919" class="mw-redirect" title="Omaha Race Riot of 1919">Omaha</a>, and two dozen other major cities in the North. The federal government did not become involved, just as it had not become involved previously.<sup id="cite_ref-326" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-326">&#91;324&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Historical_reputation">Historical reputation</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:US-$100000-GC-1934-Fr-2413.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/US-%24100000-GC-1934-Fr-2413.jpg/220px-US-%24100000-GC-1934-Fr-2413.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="196" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/US-%24100000-GC-1934-Fr-2413.jpg/330px-US-%24100000-GC-1934-Fr-2413.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/US-%24100000-GC-1934-Fr-2413.jpg/440px-US-%24100000-GC-1934-Fr-2413.jpg 2x" data-file-width="500" data-file-height="445" /></a><figcaption>A 1934 $100,000 <a href="/wiki/Gold_certificate" class="mw-redirect" title="Gold certificate">gold certificate</a> depicting Wilson</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Woodrow_Wilson_postage_stamp_issues.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/Woodrow_Wilson_postage_stamp_issues.jpeg/220px-Woodrow_Wilson_postage_stamp_issues.jpeg" decoding="async" width="220" height="76" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/Woodrow_Wilson_postage_stamp_issues.jpeg/330px-Woodrow_Wilson_postage_stamp_issues.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/Woodrow_Wilson_postage_stamp_issues.jpeg/440px-Woodrow_Wilson_postage_stamp_issues.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="1696" data-file-height="584" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/United_States_Postal_Service" title="United States Postal Service">U.S. postal</a> stamps memorializing Wilson</figcaption></figure>
<p>Wilson is generally <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">ranked by historians and political scientists</a> as an above average president.<sup id="cite_ref-jschuessler1_327-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-jschuessler1-327">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup> In the view of some historians, Wilson, more than any of his predecessors, took steps towards the creation of a strong federal government that would protect ordinary citizens against the overwhelming power of large corporations.<sup id="cite_ref-328" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-328">&#91;326&#93;</a></sup> He is generally regarded as a key figure in the establishment of <a href="/wiki/Modern_American_liberalism" class="mw-redirect" title="Modern American liberalism">modern American liberalism</a>, and a strong influence on future presidents such as <a href="/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt" title="Franklin D. Roosevelt">Franklin D. Roosevelt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson" title="Lyndon B. Johnson">Lyndon B. Johnson</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-jschuessler1_327-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-jschuessler1-327">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup> Cooper argues that in terms of impact and ambition, only the <a href="/wiki/New_Deal" title="New Deal">New Deal</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Great_Society" title="Great Society">Great Society</a> rival the domestic accomplishments of Wilson's presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-329" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-329">&#91;327&#93;</a></sup> Many of Wilson's accomplishments, including the Federal Reserve, the Federal Trade Commission, the graduated income tax, and labor laws, continued to influence the United States long after Wilson's death.<sup id="cite_ref-jschuessler1_327-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-jschuessler1-327">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Many <a href="/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States" title="Conservatism in the United States">conservatives</a> have attacked Wilson for his role in expanding the <a href="/wiki/Federal_government_of_the_United_States" title="Federal government of the United States">federal government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-330" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-330">&#91;328&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-331" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-331">&#91;329&#93;</a></sup> In 2018, conservative columnist <a href="/wiki/George_Will" title="George Will">George Will</a> wrote in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Washington_Post" title="The Washington Post">The Washington Post</a></i> that <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt" title="Theodore Roosevelt">Theodore Roosevelt</a> and Wilson were the "progenitors of today's <a href="/wiki/Imperial_presidency" title="Imperial presidency">imperial presidency</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-332" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-332">&#91;330&#93;</a></sup> Wilson's idealistic foreign policy, which came to be known as <a href="/wiki/Wilsonianism" title="Wilsonianism">Wilsonianism</a>, also cast a long shadow over <a href="/wiki/American_foreign_policy" class="mw-redirect" title="American foreign policy">American foreign policy</a>, and Wilson's League of Nations influenced the development of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-jschuessler1_327-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-jschuessler1-327">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup> Saladin Ambar writes that Wilson was "the first statesman of world stature to speak out not only against European <a href="/wiki/Imperialism" title="Imperialism">imperialism</a> but against the newer form of economic domination sometimes described as 'informal imperialism.'"<sup id="cite_ref-millerlegacy_333-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-millerlegacy-333">&#91;331&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Notwithstanding his accomplishments in office, Wilson has received criticism for his record on race relations and civil liberties, for his interventions in Latin America, and for his failure to win ratification of the Treaty of Versailles.<sup id="cite_ref-kazin1_334-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kazin1-334">&#91;332&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-millerlegacy_333-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-millerlegacy-333">&#91;331&#93;</a></sup> Despite his southern roots and record at Princeton, Wilson became the first Democrat to receive widespread support from the African-American community in a presidential election.<sup id="cite_ref-335" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-335">&#91;333&#93;</a></sup> Wilson's African-American supporters, many of whom had crossed party lines to vote for him in 1912, found themselves bitterly disappointed by the Wilson presidency, his decision to allow the imposition of Jim Crow within the federal bureaucracy in particular.<sup id="cite_ref-wolgemuth_315-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wolgemuth-315">&#91;313&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ross Kennedy writes that Wilson's support of segregation complied with predominant public opinion.<sup id="cite_ref-336" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-336">&#91;334&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/A._Scott_Berg" title="A. Scott Berg">A. Scott Berg</a> argues Wilson accepted segregation as part of a policy to "promote racial progress... by shocking the social system as little as possible."<sup id="cite_ref-337" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-337">&#91;335&#93;</a></sup> The ultimate result of this policy was unprecedented levels of segregation within the federal bureaucracy and far fewer opportunities for employment and promotion being open to African-Americans than before.<sup id="cite_ref-338" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-338">&#91;336&#93;</a></sup> Historian Kendrick Clements argues "Wilson had none of the crude, vicious racism of <a href="/wiki/James_K._Vardaman" title="James K. Vardaman">James K. Vardaman</a> or <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_R._Tillman" class="mw-redirect" title="Benjamin R. Tillman">Benjamin R. Tillman</a>, but he was insensitive to African-American feelings and aspirations."<sup id="cite_ref-339" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-339">&#91;337&#93;</a></sup> A 2021 study in the <i><a href="/wiki/Quarterly_Journal_of_Economics" class="mw-redirect" title="Quarterly Journal of Economics">Quarterly Journal of Economics</a></i> found that Wilson's segregation of the civil service increased the black-white earnings gap by 3.4–6.9 percentage points, as existing black civil servants were driven to lower-paid positions. Black civil servants who were exposed to Wilson's segregationist policies experienced a relative decline in home ownership rates, with suggestive evidence of lasting adverse effects for the descendants of those black civil servants.<sup id="cite_ref-340" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-340">&#91;338&#93;</a></sup> In the wake of the <a href="/wiki/Charleston_church_shooting" title="Charleston church shooting">Charleston church shooting</a>, some individuals demanded the removal of Wilson's name from institutions affiliated with Princeton due to his stance on race.<sup id="cite_ref-341" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-341">&#91;339&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-342" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-342">&#91;340&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Memorials">Memorials</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For a more comprehensive list, see <a href="/wiki/List_of_memorials_to_Woodrow_Wilson" title="List of memorials to Woodrow Wilson">List of memorials to Woodrow Wilson</a>.</div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Wilsonuv_pomnik_Opletalova_Praha_5876.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Wilsonuv_pomnik_Opletalova_Praha_5876.JPG/220px-Wilsonuv_pomnik_Opletalova_Praha_5876.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Wilsonuv_pomnik_Opletalova_Praha_5876.JPG/330px-Wilsonuv_pomnik_Opletalova_Praha_5876.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Wilsonuv_pomnik_Opletalova_Praha_5876.JPG/440px-Wilsonuv_pomnik_Opletalova_Praha_5876.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4272" data-file-height="2848" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_Monument" title="Woodrow Wilson Monument">Woodrow Wilson Monument</a> in <a href="/wiki/Prague" title="Prague">Prague</a>, Czech Republic</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_Presidential_Library" title="Woodrow Wilson Presidential Library">Woodrow Wilson Presidential Library</a> is located in Staunton, Virginia. The <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_Boyhood_Home" title="Woodrow Wilson Boyhood Home">Woodrow Wilson Boyhood Home</a> in <a href="/wiki/Augusta,_Georgia" title="Augusta, Georgia">Augusta, Georgia</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_House_(Washington,_D.C.)" title="Woodrow Wilson House (Washington, D.C.)">Woodrow Wilson House</a> in <a href="/wiki/Washington,_D.C." title="Washington, D.C.">Washington, D.C.</a>, are <a href="/wiki/National_Historic_Landmark" title="National Historic Landmark">National Historic Landmarks</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Woodrow_Wilson_Boyhood_Home" class="mw-redirect" title="Thomas Woodrow Wilson Boyhood Home">Thomas Woodrow Wilson Boyhood Home</a> in <a href="/wiki/Columbia,_South_Carolina" title="Columbia, South Carolina">Columbia, South Carolina</a> is listed on the <a href="/wiki/National_Register_of_Historic_Places" title="National Register of Historic Places">National Register of Historic Places</a>. <a href="/wiki/Shadow_Lawn_(New_Jersey)" title="Shadow Lawn (New Jersey)">Shadow Lawn</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Summer_White_House" class="mw-redirect" title="Summer White House">Summer White House</a> for Wilson during his term in office, became part of <a href="/wiki/Monmouth_University" title="Monmouth University">Monmouth University</a> in 1956, and was declared a National Historic Landmark in 1985. <a href="/wiki/Prospect_House_(Princeton,_New_Jersey)" title="Prospect House (Princeton, New Jersey)">Prospect House</a> in <a href="/wiki/Princeton,_New_Jersey" title="Princeton, New Jersey">Princeton, New Jersey</a>, Wilson's residence as president of <a href="/wiki/Princeton_University" title="Princeton University">Princeton University</a>, has been named a National Historic Landmark. Wilson's presidential papers and his personal library are housed in the <a href="/wiki/Library_of_Congress" title="Library of Congress">Library of Congress</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-343" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-343">&#91;341&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_International_Center_for_Scholars" title="Woodrow Wilson International Center for Scholars">Woodrow Wilson International Center for Scholars</a> in Washington, D.C., is named for Wilson, and the <a href="/wiki/Princeton_School_of_Public_and_International_Affairs" title="Princeton School of Public and International Affairs">Princeton School of Public and International Affairs</a> at Princeton University was named for Wilson until 2020 when Princeton's board of trustees voted to remove Wilson's name from the school.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_344-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-344">&#91;342&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_National_Fellowship_Foundation" class="mw-redirect" title="Woodrow Wilson National Fellowship Foundation">Woodrow Wilson National Fellowship Foundation</a> is a non-profit that provides grants for teaching fellowships. The <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_Foundation" title="Woodrow Wilson Foundation">Woodrow Wilson Foundation</a> was established to honor Wilson's legacy but was terminated in 1993. One of Princeton University's six residential colleges was originally named <a href="/wiki/Wilson_College,_Princeton_University" class="mw-redirect" title="Wilson College, Princeton University">Wilson College</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_344-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-344">&#91;342&#93;</a></sup> Numerous schools, including several <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_High_School_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Woodrow Wilson High School (disambiguation)">high schools</a>, bear Wilson's name. Several streets, including the <a href="/wiki/Rambla_Presidente_Wilson" class="mw-redirect" title="Rambla Presidente Wilson">Rambla Presidente Wilson</a> in <a href="/wiki/Montevideo" title="Montevideo">Montevideo</a>, Uruguay, have been named for Wilson. The <a href="/wiki/USS_Woodrow_Wilson_(SSBN-624)" class="mw-redirect" title="USS Woodrow Wilson (SSBN-624)">USS <i>Woodrow Wilson</i></a>, a <a href="/wiki/Lafayette-class_submarine" title="Lafayette-class submarine"><i>Lafayette</i>-class submarine</a>, was named for Wilson. Other things named for Wilson include the <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_Bridge" title="Woodrow Wilson Bridge">Woodrow Wilson Bridge</a> between <a href="/wiki/Prince_George%27s_County,_Maryland" title="Prince George&#39;s County, Maryland">Prince George's County, Maryland</a> and <a href="/wiki/Virginia" title="Virginia">Virginia</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Palais_Wilson" title="Palais Wilson">Palais Wilson</a>, which serves as the temporary headquarters of the <a href="/wiki/Office_of_the_United_Nations_High_Commissioner_for_Human_Rights" title="Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights">Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights</a> in <a href="/wiki/Geneva" title="Geneva">Geneva</a> until 2023 at the end of leasing.<sup id="cite_ref-345" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-345">&#91;343&#93;</a></sup> Monuments to Wilson include the <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_Monument" title="Woodrow Wilson Monument">Woodrow Wilson Monument</a> in <a href="/wiki/Prague" title="Prague">Prague</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-346" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-346">&#91;344&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Popular_culture">Popular culture</span></h3>
<p>In 1944, <a href="/wiki/20th_Century_Fox" class="mw-redirect" title="20th Century Fox">20th Century Fox</a> released <i><a href="/wiki/Wilson_(1944_film)" title="Wilson (1944 film)">Wilson</a></i>, a <a href="/wiki/Biopic" class="mw-redirect" title="Biopic">biopic</a> about Wilson starring <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Knox" title="Alexander Knox">Alexander Knox</a> and directed by <a href="/wiki/Henry_King_(director)" title="Henry King (director)">Henry King</a>, is considered an "idealistic" portrayal of Wilson. The movie was a personal passion project of studio president and producer <a href="/wiki/Darryl_F._Zanuck" title="Darryl F. Zanuck">Darryl F. Zanuck</a>, who was a deep admirer of Wilson. The movie was praised by film critics and Wilson supporters,<sup id="cite_ref-347" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-347">&#91;345&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-348" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-348">&#91;346&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-349" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-349">&#91;347&#93;</a></sup> and scored ten <a href="/wiki/Academy_Awards" title="Academy Awards">Academy Awards</a> nominations, winning five.<sup id="cite_ref-Erickson_1944_350-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Erickson_1944-350">&#91;348&#93;</a></sup> Despite its popularity amongst elites, <i>Wilson</i> was a <a href="/wiki/Box-office_bomb" title="Box-office bomb">box-office bomb</a>, incurring an almost $2 million loss for the studio.<sup id="cite_ref-351" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-351">&#91;349&#93;</a></sup> The movie's failure is said to have had a deep and long lasting impact on Zanuck and no attempt has been made by any major studio since to create a motion picture based around the life of Wilson.<sup id="cite_ref-Erickson_1944_350-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Erickson_1944-350">&#91;348&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

class="navbox-group" style="width:1%">1884–1900</th><td class="navbox-list-with-group navbox-list navbox-odd hlist" style="width:100%;padding:0"><div style="padding:0 0.25em">
